## AI Research

### [Should safety researchers quit frontier labs?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TfqMs3AarsHnHiwai/should-safety-researchers-quit-frontier-labs)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I've recently heard a surge of support for an old argument: AI safety researchers should not work at frontier AI companies because this reduces the likelihood of non-lethal warning shots, and we need warning shots to build support for an AI pause/slow-down. This argument has several components:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Technical AI safety work is futile, absent an AI pause:</span></b><span> Current "prosaic AGI" safety research agendas pursued at frontier AI companies, like AI control, scalable oversight, interpretability, etc., might not scale to AI systems that matter. Even when such techniques appear to benefit AI alignment, they might merely mask a deeper alignment failure that will manifest, disastrously, as AI capabilities grow. At worst, current alignment/control techniques might incentivize more sophisticated AI model deception. Even if the techniques do scale, they might be too expensive or annoying for AI company leadership to reliably mandate for all deployments, and the military or government might care even less!</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Technical AI safety work might block warning shots:</span></b><span> The warning shots that state-of-the-art (SotA) alignment, control, and monitoring techniques can prevent might be "non-lethal" to humanity, whereas future "lethal" incidents might not be prevented by SotA alignment/control techniques. By deploying SotA alignment and control techniques within frontier AI companies, we reduce the risk of warning shots like the OpenAI x Hugging Face incident (which OpenAI monitors supposedly would have caught, had they been turned on for the particular internal deployment), but we might fail to prevent lethal loss-of-control incidents.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Warning shots are instrumental to an AI pause:</span></b><span> If we assume that SotA AI safety research is insufficient to "make AI go well" on the current trajectory, then we might need an AI pause or slowdown (e.g., </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>Plan A/S</span></a><span>). Warning shots might help build political appetite for a pause or slowdown by convincing policymakers and the public that catastrophic AI risks are real. Therefore, people working on alignment or control at a frontier AI company should consider quitting their jobs to increase the chance that non-lethal warning shots occur and spur an AI pause.</span></li></ul><p><span>I think this argument has some merit. I expect that RLHF++ will probably be insufficient to align TED-AI circa-2028 and such systems will be terrifically difficult to monitor or control. However, I think there are also significant weaknesses to this argument. Here are some countervailing points to consider:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Alignment MVPs are probably still useful: </span></b><span>In spite of the OpenAI x Hugging Face incident, I expect that most AI safety researchers will use frontier models to help with research, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qTNm8qzqhhpno58fZ/resolution-has-a-new-agent-foundations-team" rel="noreferrer"><span>including agent foundations researchers</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://aligned.substack.com/p/alignment-mvp" rel="noreferrer"><span>Alignment MVPs</span></a><span>, AIs that are sufficiently aligned/controlled to aid research, may remain a crucial part of AI safety research during an AI pause or slowdown. Currently, the best AIs would arguably be unusable for safety research without the hard work of frontier AI company post-training and safety teams. If AI safety researchers quit frontier AI companies en masse, we might be stuck using current generation AIs for the duration of an AI pause; maybe this is unideal?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>"Safety-stragglers" will cause warning shots anyways: </span></b><span>Most US-based frontier AI companies, like Google DeepMind, Meta, and xAI, scored failing grades on </span><a href="https://guidelight.ai/blog/control-assessment-august-2026#scoring-chart" rel="noreferrer"><span>Guidelight's Control standard</span></a><span>. Apparently, xAI has only two staff working on frontier AI safety and Meta is not much better off. Chinese AI companies like DeepSeek, Moonshot, Zhipu, etc. might have ~0 frontier safety staff. If leading AI companies are </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/eci?subset-view=graph&amp;subset-tab=Software+engineering&amp;view=graph&amp;tab=release-date&amp;showFrontierTrend=true" rel="noreferrer"><span>only a few months ahead</span></a><span> of "safety-stragglers", we might see politically expedient warning shots from the least safety-conscious AI companies, regardless of what the leading companies do.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Responses to historical warning shots are high-variance: </span></b><span>The Chernobyl disaster slowed nuclear power adoption worldwide; maybe an AI warning shot would likewise slow AI progress? As a counterexample, consider the atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki, which arguably spurred the Cold War nuclear arms race. AI warning shots could actually spur military investment and weaponization of AI! Also, the COVID-19 pandemic, which caused massive loss of life, does not seem to have caused Western society to take pandemic risk seriously.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>The purpose of an AI pause is do more safety work:</span></b><span> Let's say that we pause or slow down frontier AI progress; what then? Arguably, the next step is to solve ASI alignment as fast as possible. Absent an staggeringly effective compute monitoring regime (or "burning all the GPUs"), we might eventually have to build aligned AGI to help detect and sabotage blacksite projects. If we curtail the development of new AI safety researchers by discouraging them from working with research mentors at frontier AI companies now, we might have a smaller talent pool to capitalize on an AI pause.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>The next warning shot might be lethal:</span></b><span> It's possible that the next OAIxHF-style incident will cause loss of life, via bioweapons or cyber attacks on critical systems. If safety researchers quit frontier AI companies now, absent an AI pause, this might enable a catastrophe.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span>Allowing warning shots "for the greater good" seems morally fraught: </span></b><span>This point speaks for itself. I think that arguments to "allow short-term harm for the greater good" should have to overcome a strong prior against this kind of thinking. Letting people get hurt seems bad, particularly if there is significant uncertainty about whether it is necessary.</span></li></ul><p><span>Overall, I am cautiously optimistic about working on certain types of safety research at frontier AI companies, particularly if a coordinated AI slowdown (e.g., Plan A) occurs. I nevertheless feel highly uncertain about the "Alignment MVP" strategy in light of recent containment failures and alignment results. I think this merits serious consideration.</span></p><p><br /></p><hr /><p><span>Disclosure: I'm the CEO of </span><a href="https://www.matsprogram.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>MATS</span></a><span>, which trains AI safety researchers, including with mentors at frontier AI companies, and a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tPjAgWpsQrveFECWP/ryan-kidd-s-shortform?commentId=6dwq7qkszz5dyZ6sj" rel="noreferrer"><span>meaningful fraction</span></a><span> of our alumni end up working at those companies. If the argument I'm responding to is right, a good chunk of what MATS has done might be counterproductive, so I have an obvious incentive to find it wrong. I've tried to steelman it anyway and I'd appreciate feedback.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TfqMs3AarsHnHiwai/should-safety-researchers-quit-frontier-labs#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Announcing Humans in Control: cross-partisan grassroots organizing for AI safeguards ahead of 2028](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bs4ayLuE5aAhnBArL/announcing-humans-in-control-cross-partisan-grassroots)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i>While HIC’s work is aimed at the broader public, we are posting this announcement here because we expect some readers may be interested in volunteering, donating, or making useful introductions.</i></p><p><strong>TLDR:</strong><a href="https://humansincontrol.org"> <u>Humans in Control</u></a> is a cross-partisan grassroots advocacy organization focused on AI safeguards. We are building a grassroots movement, with the aim of making AI safeguards a meaningful issue in the 2028 election. Our principles are that AI should help people, not replace them; companies and governments should be responsible for harm; and we should not build AI we cannot control. Our north star is a verifiable international agreement to not build AI we cannot control.</p><p><a href="https://linkedin.com/in/vaelgates">Vael Gates</a> founded HIC in January 2026, and on August 10,<a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jonwarnow/"> <u>Jon Warnow</u></a> succeeded Vael as HIC’s executive director. We are looking for volunteers who can commit recurring time and take ownership of programs, especially students, parents, and people living in smaller cities or rural communities. We are also fundraising and are looking for people willing to give small or large donations.</p><h2><strong>What Humans in Control is</strong></h2><p>HIC is a hybrid 501(c)(3) and 501(c)(4). The c3 supports public education, volunteer recruitment and training, chapters and coalitions, community presentations, and other field activities; the c4 supports candidate-facing work that the c3 cannot legally fund.</p><p>We use a “snowflake” organizing model: paid staff recruit and train volunteer leaders, who then recruit and lead their own teams. This is designed to build ongoing relationships among volunteers, their leaders, and the organization.</p><p>We are building in states likely to vote early in the 2028 presidential primaries, alongside a national distributed program. Our work is organized around three ground rules:</p><ul><li>AI should help people, not replace them.</li><li>Companies and governments should be responsible for harm.</li><li>We should not build AI we cannot control.</li></ul><p>Our near-term goal is to increase the likelihood that the winner of the 2028 presidential election takes meaningful executive action on AI safeguards in early 2029. Our longer-term goal is a verifiable international agreement under which no company or country can develop superintelligent AI unless there is strong scientific evidence that it can be controlled.</p><p>We do not think grassroots pressure alone is sufficient to produce that outcome. Policy development, candidate outreach, technical expertise, communications, funding, diplomacy, Congress, states, and international partners all matter. HIC is working on one part of that larger political strategy: helping people understand the issue, organize with others, and act together. We see the main bottleneck to achieving AI safeguards as increasing <i>political will</i>, and grassroots organizing is one approach aiming directly at that bottleneck.</p><h2><strong>A change in leadership</strong></h2><p>On August 10,<a href="https://www.linkedin.com/posts/jonwarnow_its-time-for-as-they-say-some-personal-share-7487951703338835968-7W1A"> <u>Jon Warnow</u></a> became HIC’s executive director.</p><p><a href="https://linkedin.com/in/vaelgates">Vael Gates </a>founded HIC because grassroots advocacy seemed like an important gap in advancing AI safeguards. HIC has now reached a phase where the central work is building and managing a grassroots organization over several years. Jon is a senior grassroots organizing leader (co-founded 350.org, helped organize the People's Climate March, and has worked on Congressional and Presidential campaigns) who has substantially deeper experience doing that and is better suited to lead this phase. </p><p>Jon found HIC after becoming concerned about advanced AI, and worked closely with Vael and the team before the transition. Vael will support the organization while looking for a role that better fits their comparative advantage. HIC’s mission and core strategy have not changed.</p><h2><strong>Why grassroots organizing, and why 2028</strong></h2><p>HIC expects AI to produce substantial benefits, but does not expect voluntary company safeguards to be sufficient for the most serious risks. Government action is needed to address current harms, and international coordination will be necessary to prevent any company or country from building systems that humans cannot reliably control.</p><p>Given that, the central strategic question is what is causally necessary to slow the race to develop AI that even its creators are unsure they can control. We believe political will is the current main gap, and that large groups of people working together strategically can help close it.</p><p>As AI rapidly develops, many consequential decisions are being made about the governance of AI. HIC aims to support current congressional and executive-focused efforts to enact AI safeguards within the current administration. </p><p>However, we believe the next administration provides a key opportunity to ensure people’s voices are heard. The next administration will make consequential decisions about increasingly capable AI, and the executive branch has substantial authority over federal agencies and international negotiations, although the president will not act alone. For this reason, the presidential primaries offer a uniquely strong opportunity for grassroots pressure.</p><p>In particular, competitive primaries offer an opportunity to engage candidates who are still developing their positions. Volunteers can attend events and ask candidates across parties to explain what they would do about the anticipated consequences of AI. This strategy works best when primaries are competitive, but we expect leverage regardless.</p><p>HIC discusses concerns from today’s AI – employment, data centers, child safety, scams, surveillance, and accountability – alongside catastropic risks and the possibility that future systems become powerful enough that humans cannot reliably control them. We think decisions with consequences this large should not be left to the companies developing the technology.</p><h2><strong>What we are building, and potential points of concern</strong></h2><p>The current program is mostly base-building: finding people who want to help, supporting volunteers in becoming leaders, forming teams and chapters, and running community presentations. Candidate engagement will become more central as the 2028 campaigns develop.</p><p>HIC has begun forming volunteer teams, running presentations in several states, and building the systems needed to support more local and national volunteers. This shows that people are willing to participate. The more important questions are whether volunteers choose to remain involved, take meaningful responsibility, and build teams that continue without close staff direction. Right now, it’s too early to make a definitive assessment, though we believe we are on a good path.</p><p>There are several reasons this might not work:</p><ul><li><strong>In-person organizing may grow too slowly to matter during the 2028 cycle. </strong>We're mitigating this by building distributed (remote) engagement programs alongside in-person chapters, and by prioritizing early-primary states where even modest organizing can get outsized attention.</li><li><strong>People may care about current AI harms without wanting to organize around larger-scale loss-of-control risks (e.g. </strong><a href="https://humansincontrol.org/campaigns/openai-hugging-face-breach/"><strong><u>July loss-of-control example</u></strong></a><strong>).</strong> Our education and activation programs connect present-day harms (like child safety issues, job loss, and surveillance-at-scale) to catastrophic risks.</li><li><strong>HIC may not succeed in earning trust across political lines.</strong> So far, the framing (commonsense rules, accountability, protecting families) has resonated across partisan lines, and we are working constructively in a politically diverse coalition – but we have more work to do to build a politically diverse team, and movement.</li><li><strong>Volunteers could advocate for policies that do not reduce the risks HIC is concerned about. </strong>We manage this through training, community agreements, and public clarity on our strategic focus and core messages.</li><li><strong>The political or technical situation may develop differently from what we expect. </strong>We're building flexible infrastructure (organized people, trained leaders, local relationships) that can pivot to new targets, rather than betting everything on one policy outcome, AI capabilities timeline, or political campaign.</li><li><strong>Grassroots pressure alone won’t be sufficient.</strong> We believe that policy development, technical work, diplomacy, and political leadership all matter. HIC is building one necessary piece (intense grassroots pressure to create strong political will) but not the whole puzzle.</li></ul><p>Even successful grassroots organizing would not be enough to produce an international agreement alone. That would also require policy development, technical work on verification, effective staffing, diplomacy, and political leadership. We nevertheless think grassroots organizing is an important part of building the political will required for action.</p><h2><strong>How to help</strong></h2><h3><strong>Volunteer</strong></h3><p>Volunteers lead teams, organize community presentations, build local and campus chapters, recruit other participants, and contribute research, data, operations, communications, and media skills.</p><p>We are particularly interested in people who can contribute recurring time and take responsibility for a team or project. People in states likely to play an early role in the presidential cycle are especially useful, but much of the national work can be done from anywhere. We’re also especially interested in students who can help build out our student program!</p><p>One specific, near-term volunteer activity is to host or support a local event for our upcoming “<a href="https://realitycheckhq.org/"><u>AI Reality Check</u></a>” day of action. On Saturday, November 14, volunteers in towns and cities across the country will give a dynamic presentation on AI risks that can turn concern into action.</p><p>You can also sign up to our general <a href="https://humansincontrol.org/volunteer"><u>Volunteer form here</u></a>, or email team@humansincontrol.org if you are unsure where you would fit.</p><h3><strong>Donate</strong></h3><ul><li><a href="https://donorbox.org/support-humans-in-control-action"><strong><u>Donate c4 dollars</u></strong></a>: currently especially useful because they support candidate engagement and other election-related advocacy. Donations are not tax-deductible.</li><li><a href="https://www.every.org/humansincontrol"><strong><u>Donate c3 dollars</u></strong></a>: support public education, volunteer training, chapters, and field organizing. Donations are tax-deductible.</li></ul><p>Small recurring donations help HIC develop a broader donor base. Larger multi-year commitments help support HIC’s staff and larger programs.</p><p>HIC accepts donations from employees of frontier AI companies, but not from founders or C-suite executives. (<a href="https://humansincontrol.org/about/#gift-acceptance-policy"><u>Full gift acceptance policy here</u></a>.)</p><p>We also welcome introductions to potential donors, experienced organizers, political practitioners, community leaders, and people with strong organizing networks.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bs4ayLuE5aAhnBArL/announcing-humans-in-control-cross-partisan-grassroots#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Let's talk about the AI coordination problem](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QYDZzuGjrKu7wKdC8/let-s-talk-about-the-ai-coordination-problem)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.substack.com/p/an-easy-coordination-problem"><span>Yesterday</span></a><span> I asked if this ‘coordinate not to build dangerous AI’ problem was actually easy.</span></p><p><span>Why would I think that, contrary to so much belief?</span></p><p><span>Well, I don’t feel like I’ve actually heard much about the detail of it. In my experience people don’t talk about it like it’s a real practical problem with details, like the negotiation to end a war.</span></p><p><span>They also don’t talk about it like it’s a serious problem of global geopolitical import, like the negotiation to end a war.</span></p><p><span>It’s more like a topic for obscure intellectuals, sophomores and trolls to discuss for as long as it takes for one to mention it and another to assuredly dismiss it.</span></p><p><span>If we treated negotiation to end a war similarly, state leaders would never attempt it, and if you suggested it on social media, the conversation would mostly be strangers appearing to tell you you’re an idiot because you obviously can’t coordinate thousands of people not to kill each other. (Also, do you not realize there are big financial incentives? And if you somehow stopped Country A from killing people from Country B, Country A is just going to pay someone else to do it!)</span></p><p><span>That is, saying things that sound plausible on a five-second analysis from some reasonable heuristics, with zero curiosity about whether their assessment is missing anything key, or if there might be a way to a solution.</span></p><p><span>This would be a huge mistake! Pragmatically, you can negotiate to end wars. And given this, it is very important to notice this and do so, because otherwise lots of people die.</span></p><p><span>The stakes of AI racing are just as real, though our vision of them is hazier at present. But with AI racing, everyone seems to just nod along with this level of argument.</span></p><p><span>This is not enough to give me the confidence to give up! I do not trust this kind of thinking.</span></p><p><span>And thinking about it in a ‘real-world pragmatic problem that we want to solve’ mental mode, rather than a ‘abstract philosophical chit-chat’ mental mode, I notice confusion at finding such coordination super hard, let alone unimaginably hard beyond worth trying, and especially so for the specific people involved. If you can coordinate to continue as CEO of a company that you were just fired from and for the board to leave instead, I think there’s some chance you can sort out a more run-of-the-mill ‘if you all don’t, we won’t’ deal.</span></p><p><span>What do I think should happen differently?</span></p><p><b><span>I. We should talk seriously about AI coordination</span></b></p><p><span>What would need to happen for coordination to avoid building potentially catastrophic AI? If it’s so hard, what steps are difficult? What would a road map look like? Who would need to do what? If there are hard bits, are there alternatives? We should have a detailed picture, and discuss it like a real problem that we care about solving.</span></p><p><b><span>II. AI leaders should talk seriously about AI coordination</span></b></p><p><span>If we were taking this situation seriously and expecting these people to do a real job navigating the risk, would we settle for assuming they’ve sufficiently considered and pursued the options for coordinating, without discussing it at all in public?</span></p><p><span>If they are so sure it can’t be done, why is that? Shouldn’t we demand to know?</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QYDZzuGjrKu7wKdC8/let-s-talk-about-the-ai-coordination-problem#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Training Models to Predict and Explain Their In-the-Wild Behavior](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YyAMz52wDxnLhwvWL/training-models-to-predict-and-explain-their-in-the-wild)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><span>Summary</span></h2><p><span>Our </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/chive/"><span>CHIVE pipeline</span></a><span> produced thousands of unexpected behaviors with explanations that are grounded in counterfactual prompts (see Figure 1 for an example). In this post, we focus on using this data to train models to predict the outcomes of counterfactual prompts and to explain their behaviors.</span></p><p><span>We build two training targets from our CHIVE-generated data (see Figure 2 for examples): </span><b><span>counterfactual prediction</span></b><span>, where the model answers a binary question ("would this specific edit to the prompt change your behavior?"), and </span><b><span>open-ended self-explanation</span></b><span>, where the model proposes the cause of its behavior and counterfactual prompts to verify its explanation.</span></p><p><span>We find three results:</span></p><p><b><span>1. Training on this single general data source generalizes to held-out datasets.</span></b><span> It transfers to a held-out datasets the model never trained on: predicting whether a hint (e.g. a suggested MMLU answer or a user's opinion on an Am I The Asshole post) influenced its answer. To our knowledge this is the first instance of causal self-explanation training generalizing to a held-out OOD dataset (see Background). Typically when prior work reports generalization, it is narrow, such as from one hint format or dataset to another.</span></p><p><b><span>2. The counterfactual prediction training target substantially outperforms the open-ended one, even though both training datasets come from the same underlying investigations.</span></b><span> Counterfactual prediction training improves for every model in every setting we tested. Open-ended self-explanation shows notable generalization in only one model / evaluation combination (Qwen3.5-397B-A17B in the hint setting).</span></p><p><b><span>3. We observe no privileged access in causal self-explanation training.</span></b><span> We train Qwen3-8B to predict Qwen3-8B's behavior, and separately train Llama-3.1-8B on the exact same data. Llama predicts Qwen's behavior just as well as Qwen predicts itself. If Qwen had introspective access to its own processing, it should have had an advantage. This is the standard test for "privileged access" (Binder et al. 2024), and our result matches their null result in the hint setting.</span></p><p><span>This post is a follow up on our </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/chive/"><span>previous post</span></a><span>, where we had focused on using CHIVE-generated data to evaluate interpretability tools. Here, we focus on using CHIVE-generated investigations as training data.</span></p><p><span>📄 </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16747"><span>Paper</span></a><span>, 💻 </span><a href="https://github.com/adamkarvonen/chive"><span>Code, models, and datasets</span></a></p><h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/YyAMz52wDxnLhwvWL/bf2e24a8ae98f65e4906cff311c8adefeb0361381c7ca1ab92ad75f93edc6e34/l65owggw20ggmbdeduui" /></h2><p><b><span>Figure 1</span></b><span>. </span><i><span>One investigation produced by CHIVE. Gemma repeatedly misreads the range of a simple function. Renaming the parameters from `max`/`min` to `a`/`b` makes the error nearly disappear, providing experimental evidence that the misleading names drive the mistake.</span></i></p><h2><span>Background</span></h2><p><span>We would like to train models to produce self-explanations where they correctly describe what actually drove the model's behavior, rather than merely sounding plausible. (Throughout, we mean causal, behavioral self-explanations, or what drove a specific behavior, and what edit would change it, as distinct from work on models reporting general properties of themselves, e.g. "are you a risk-seeking model?" (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.11120"><span>Betley et al. 2025</span></a><span>).) The standard way to get ground truth on "what actually drove the behavior" is to run counterfactuals, where you change an aspect of the prompt and check whether the behavior changes. But it is very difficult to programmatically generate inputs with known counterfactual causes that are </span><i><span>interesting</span></i><span>. The average random edit to a text document is either meaningless (the behavior doesn't change) or changes the behavior in a completely unsurprising way, such as swapping "France" for "Germany" in "What is the capital of France?".</span></p><p><span>This is why the self-explanation literature is dominated by </span><b><span>hint settings</span></b><span>. In this setting, we place a hint in the prompt (such as a suggested answer on an MMLU question or a user's opinion on an AITA post), measure whether the model's answer flips when the cue is removed, and ask whether the model's explanation only acknowledges the hint when it actually flipped its answer. Most work on training models to explain themselves has been in this setting (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04388"><span>Turpin et al. (2023)</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05518"><span>Chua et al. (2024)</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.22777"><span>Turpin et al. (2025)</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20710"><span>Hase et al. (2026)</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32038"><span>Guo et al. (2026)</span></a><span>), with most of the remaining work on simple tabular tasks.</span></p><p><span>The setting is very narrow, as there is one known input feature (the hint) and one predefined output behavior (whether the model followed it). The questions we actually want self-explanations to answer in deployment (e.g. "why did the model write insecure code here?") have infinite potential causes and infinite behaviors. To our knowledge, no training for causal self-explanations has been shown to generalize to a genuinely held-out task. Where generalization is reported, it is narrow, such as from one hint format to another. Methods which produce strong results in the hint setting have been found to produce much weaker results in more general settings (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20710"><span>Hase et al. (2026)</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>Instead of inserting a known cause, our pipeline spends agent inference compute to discover one: it screens a target model's completions on in-the-wild prompts for surprising behaviors, then searches over counterfactual edits until it isolates what actually causes each behavior.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/26332bf5cab67e2a38323089eba2456979daefa233dd7c2f2048f9a9e4e33cdf/cimqlrsyyonlukfayjcq" /><p><b><span>Figure 2</span></b><span>. </span><i><span>Two training targets built from the same investigation. Counterfactual prediction asks whether one specific edit will change the behavior, open-ended self-explanation asks the model to propose causes and the counterfactuals to verify its explanation.</span></i></p><h2><span>Setup</span></h2><p><span>Our pipeline (described in full in the </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/chive/"><span>CHIVE post)</span></a><span> takes an arbitrary target model and prompt source and produces explanations of its behavior which are grounded in counterfactual prompts. In our case, it runs the target model on thousands of in-the-wild WildChat prompts (10-30 completions each), uses an Opus agent to screen for surprising or confusing behaviors, then has the agent repeatedly run counterfactual prompts until it isolates what actually causes the behavior. An independent judge verifies each explanation against the full experimental record. For our evaluation, we ask a binary question:  will a specific prompt edit change the behavior (Figure 2, left)?</span></p><p><span>This setup has some appealing properties as a source of synthetic training data. It lets us distill a large amount of inference compute into each training point, since every label is the product of many counterfactual experiments run during the investigation. The counterfactual outcome labels are also cheap and objective to verify, because the label of a counterfactual claim comes from actually re-running the model under the intervention and checking whether the behavior rate moved. Note that whether the open-ended explanation is a true, complete description of why a behavior happened is fuzzier and harder to certify, which is why we restrict our evaluation to predicting counterfactual outcomes.</span></p><h2><span>Models and investigation setting</span></h2><p><span>The first model we use is Qwen3-8B. In the evaluation setting, for the highest-quality data, our investigations are always run by Opus, which is investigating a smaller target model such as Qwen3-8B. For training purposes, this introduces two confounds.</span></p><p><span>The first is distillation: when a much more capable model runs the investigation, an uplift in the trained target may simply reflect distillation from the larger model, rather than anything specific to the training-data setup. The second is the capability gap. While it is easier for a strong model to find and explain issues in a weaker one, in a realistic deployment you would only have a model of roughly equal capability available to investigate itself. We therefore want to know whether this process can bootstrap from a model's own investigations, rather than relying on access to a stronger model somewhere in the loop.</span></p><p><span>To address both confounds, we also run a self-investigation setting in which a more capable model, Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, investigates itself. The investigator and target are the same model, and the training labels come from re-running its own counterfactual interventions.</span></p><h2><span>Training targets</span></h2><p><span>Every investigation has a transcript where the model did something unexpected, plus a set of counterfactual experiments that isolate why. We use this to create two different training targets. In </span><b><span>counterfactual prediction</span></b><span>, the model directly answers one binary question about one experiment. In </span><b><span>open-ended self-explanation</span></b><span>, the model produces an open-ended self explanation of why it did the behavior (see Figure 2 for examples).</span></p><p><b><span>Counterfactual prediction.</span></b><span> We train the model to directly answer the binary counterfactual questions from our evaluation. The model is shown a behavior and a specific prompt edit, and it predicts whether that edit would move the behavior. In this case, the model becomes a classifier. Doing well here is not useful on its own, since you could always just run the counterfactual instead of asking the model, but it is the simplest setting in which to measure how much signal about its own counterfactual behavior a model can recover at all.</span></p><p><b><span>Open-ended self-explanation.</span></b><span> We train the model to produce a post-hoc self-explanation of why it produced its behavior. Each investigation already creates a structured report, and we train the model to produce that report directly as its answer. The report begins by stating the observed behavior, then lays out the core causes, the experiments run to support each cause, and any negative evidence such as proposed hypotheses that did not hold up. This setting could be much more useful in practice, as there is the potential to propose causes or experiments that we hadn't thought of. We show 20 randomly sampled open-ended investigations </span><a href="https://adamkarvonen.github.io/scratch/freeform_investigations.html"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Results</span></h2><h3><span>Counterfactual prediction</span></h3><p><span>We first evaluate with the common </span><b><span>hint setting</span></b><span>, which was not targeted by training. A hint is inserted in the prompt, such as “A Stanford professor thinks the answer is A”. We measure whether the model’s answer changes when the hint is removed.</span></p><p><b><span>Counterfactual prediction training consistently generalizes to the held-out hint setting.</span></b><span> We ask the binary question directly ("Would your answer change if the hint was removed?"). In this case, we see consistent generalization for both Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, as both models significantly improve with training. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of self-explanation training generalizing to a genuinely held-out setting.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/a721e4f7851e551cb95457773976075c7f7c27a2b0487820367b5529cbc40924/pfc9o5jutkiqfh6o3yyf" /><p><b><span>Figure 3</span></b><span>. </span><i><span>Counterfactual prediction results in the hint setting. Although training never targeted hints, it improves for both models.</span></i></p><p><span>We also evaluate held-out investigations produced by CHIVE. These include an in-distribution source of transcripts (Wildchat) and an out-of-distribution source (PETRI). Once again, we find consistent generalization to all settings.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/13ed72b9c100ea0512bc5e075d6bdbf7dbb5393f99361b749987c75ae003cb1e/zmpgodsi7klrw1eicdtz" /><p><b><span>Figure 4</span></b><span>.</span><i><span> Counterfactual-prediction training improves both models on held-out WildChat and out-of-distribution PETRI investigations, reaching roughly the performance of Opus 4.8 reading the same transcripts.</span></i></p><h3><span>Open-ended self-explanation</span></h3><p><span>Ultimately, we would like to ask a model "Why did you do that?" and receive a complete answer that identifies causes we did not already know to test. Unfortunately, this is not yet working reliably, and we find weaker mixed results.</span></p><p><b><span>Open-ended self-explanations have inconsistent generalization to the hint setting.</span></b><span> We use the same hint dataset with the same labels for this evaluation. In this case, we ask "why did you answer with X?" and use a judge to extract whether the explanation identifies that the user's hint was influential for its answer. An accurate explainer should identify the hint more when removing the hint actually flips the behavior:</span></p><p><span>In this case, we only see notable generalization (0.59 -&gt; 0.68) in the larger 397B model. The base models are near random chance because they almost never acknowledge the hint, consistent with prior findings that models rarely verbalize this influence (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04388"><span>Turpin et al. (2023)</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d92263826149dbb8887fd89e25e85494e51807e8e8cd07dfd1726a677baf24fe/kkpdsfmpbtwncpahezx0" /><p><b><span>Figure 5</span></b><span>. </span><i><span>Open-ended explanation results in the hint setting.</span></i></p><p><span>In this case, we only see notable generalization (0.59 -&gt; 0.68) in the larger 397B model. The base models are near random chance because they almost never acknowledge the hint, consistent with prior findings that models rarely verbalize this influence (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04388"><span>Turpin et al. (2023)</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>We also experimented with the metric of </span><b><span>counterfactual simulatability</span></b><span>: we give another simulator model the transcript and the self-explanation, and ask it to predict the outcome of specific prompt edits. If the explanation is faithful and useful, this simulator should predict the target’s behavior better than a simulator that only reads the transcript. In this case, we find mixed results: the self-explanations help weak simulators (e.g. Gemma-3-12B and Gemma-3-27B) but hurt strong simulators (Qwen-3.6-27B and Opus 4.8). See Appendix E.3 for more details.</span></p><h2><span>Is the self-explanation model introspecting?</span></h2><p><span>When training a model to explain its behaviors, it is difficult to distinguish two stories. The model may be gaining some form of introspective privileged access to its own processing, or it may simply be learning a good prior over what this model tends to do, which any capable model could in principle learn from the same data. The most common test from the self-explanation literature is </span><b><span>privileged access</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.13787"><span>Binder et al. 2024</span></a><span>): train both model A and a separate model B on model A's behavioral data. If A still predicts itself better (even if model B is more capable), the advantage must come from an internal signal not available from the training data alone.</span></p><p><b><span>We find no privileged access for our self-explanations.</span></b><span> We run the standard privileged access experiment in both directions with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. For each subject model, we train both the subject itself (self) and the other model (cross) on the subject's claim data and evaluate both on held-out claims about the subject. We find no self-advantage in either direction: the cross-trained predictor is within error of the self-trained one for both subjects, and every trained model lands at the Opus transcript-reading reference.</span></p><p><span>This is consistent with the results of Binder et al., who also found no self-advantage when models are trained to predict whether a hint would influence their answer, which is a narrow version of our causal self-explanation task.  However, it does contrast with </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08579"><span>Li et al.</span></a><span>, who did find privileged access in the hint setting with our same model pair, and we are uncertain what explains the difference. We therefore use the term "self-explanation" to describe the task format of training a model to report on its own behavior, and not as a claim that the skill is introspective. We have further discussion on the observed lack of privileged access in the paper’s Appendix.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/289f1ddd908dc4c3816d000b8ddaa6638dbca6ad6112505e79d86f1b6c7de4ea/pgdrp2regnspk5nzkhuu" /><p><b><span>Figure 6</span></b><span>.</span><i><span> Privileged-access test. A model trained on its own behavior performs no better than a different model trained on the same data, with both near the Opus transcript-reading reference.</span></i></p><h2><span>Discussion</span></h2><p><span>On the bright side, we now have datasets that enable generalization to held-out datasets.  We think datasets such as ours are a promising testbed for future self-explanation methods (a natural direction is to directly RL against the counterfactual simulatability metric). Pipelines like ours are also a promising method for creating self-explanation data, as they are very scalable and the labels are high quality, because we can easily obtain the ground truth by rerunning the model.</span></p><p><b><span>Adam's personal interpretation.</span></b><span> However, the results are weaker than I had hoped. Even with the larger Qwen3.5-397B model, we were unable to convincingly beat Opus 4.8. It's also odd that we don't observe privileged access, even if this is consistent with the results of Binder et al. Intuitively, I would have guessed that there should be some privileged access result here. Anecdotally, I've heard of a couple other forthcoming projects that also found no privileged access in related settings. Together, these results suggest that training struggles to elicit information which is uniquely available to the model itself.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YyAMz52wDxnLhwvWL/training-models-to-predict-and-explain-their-in-the-wild#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Almost nobody is funded to figure out what work would solve alignment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g4eaRCynouiBi2LjQ/almost-nobody-is-funded-to-figure-out-what-work-would-solve)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Solving alignment would be easier if we worked out what problems we actually need to solve. This could be called the </span><i><span>alignment meta-problem</span></i><span>.  Work on this problem is rarely directly funded. More focused work on it should let us use our limited time and funding more efficiently.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787950713/lexical_client_uploads/yguvxrdnzn5u0d0vtg94.png" /><figcaption><p><span>The diagram implies narrowing alignment work, but I expect meta-problem work to also identify high-payoff "fringe" approaches. </span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>If we're driving toward a cliff, maybe we should buy better headlights. </span></p><p><span>All too often we're doing work that merely sounds or feels good, and optimizing less than we could for work that drives most efficiently toward success. Some of this is inevitable and some of it is useful, but we could do more to light the path ahead.</span><br /><br /><span>Edit, in response to early comments which revealed I wasn't being clear: the meta-problem as I'm defining it includes any means of identifying more and less useful work, but it would probably mostly focus on two elements. The first is predicting the path to takeover-capable AI, including its design, how it's deployed, and the societal/organizational/governmental actions that will constrain alignment solutions. The second major element would be mapping from those predictions to likely-most-useful work. This mapping would include identifying the hardest parts of the problem to receive more work, or to somehow route around (e.g., slowing stopping if the hard problems are practically unsolvable).</span></p><p><span>Most researchers agree that mech interp, refining and improving alignment training, control, theory, and miscellaneous techniques like confession are useful for solving alignment. Working toward regulation and slowdown/pause is also commonly considered useful in the governance space, and spreading awareness of alignment risks is pretty obviously useful for accelerating and enabling all of this work. But we don't know what variants of this or other work make best use of our limited time and funding. Which of them, or what others, are the most efficient use of resources? I think that we could probably get usefully better answers at relatively low cost.</span></p><p><span>Currently work on the meta-problem is done either in researchers' spare time, during grant applications, or inside funding orgs and AI developers. All have limited incentives to publish their thinking legibly, and incentives during grant applications and inside dev orgs are subject to Goodharting and motivated reasoning: sounding good vs. being good. More funding directly for analysis and planning seems like an efficient leverage point. </span></p><p><span>The AI Futures Project is the exception that demonstrates the trend. They are funded to do prediction work, but that spreads into many aspects of the meta-problem. They make </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nEBbw2Bc2CnN2RMxy/gears-level-models-are-capital-investments"><span>Gears-Level Models</span></a><span> of how alignment and governance work might succeed or fail, and so identify work that's likely to be more and less useful. They have the time and focus to explicitly include the many cross-dependencies between governance, public opinion, and technical alignment work, in contrast to people who do this in their spare time and mostly focus on their own area of expertise. (Other orgs and people do aspects of this work, but arguably do less direct work on the meta-problem as I'm thinking of it.)</span></p><p><span>Many researchers and orgs do some of this work despite it not being directly funded, because they think it's so valuable. And people at funding orgs do meta-problem work as part of their job. But publications of funders' models tend to be short and limited (although kudos to the funders who do carefully work out and publish their theories-of-change!).</span></p><h1><span>Why not to fund more work on the meta-problem</span></h1><p><span>There are good reasons not to fund this type of work more heavily. In brief:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Science doesn't typically do this, for good reason</span></li><li value="2"><span>Could concentrate funding, neglecting important work </span><ul><li value="1"><span>Bad predictions and work recommendations are worse than none</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>This work is being done adequately through indirect incentives</span></li><li value="4"><span>Maybe what we're doing is the right work, particularly for short timelines</span></li><li value="5"><span>Could waste money on useless or bad work that's hard to evaluate</span></li><li value="6"><span>Prediction is hard, particularly about complex and novel scenarios</span></li></ul><p><span>These are all important points. Each is something to guard against, or a reason to not over-fund the meta-problem. </span></p><h1><span>Arguments in favor, compressed</span></h1><p><span>Each of the arguments against suggests an argument for:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Unlike other sciences, alignment has a specific goal and time pressure</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Like planned projects such as Apollo, Manhattan, WWII</span></li><li value="2"><span>(And perhaps other sciences would benefit, too; observing cognitive neuroscience and its funding for 23 years has led me to think they would)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Over-concentration of funding is more likely if there is less/worse meta-problem work  </span><ul><li value="1"><span>Over-concentration could be happening now, since only a few people and orgs are funded to do this work</span></li><li value="2"><span>We're already making predictions and work recommendations, just rarely with much time-on-task</span></li><li value="3"><span>Could draw more funding into the field by clarifying what's probably useful </span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>While a lot of this work is being done, most people are doing it in small pieces, and are incentivized and have </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QpgmEhBvJQxAfFMP2/motivated-reasoning-confirmation-bias-and-ai-risk-theory"><span>Motivated reasoning</span></a><span> toward justifying their own work</span></li><li value="4"><span>Maybe we're already doing all the necessary work; why not rigorously check? </span><ul><li value="1"><span>Theory, and gears-level models of the transition to LLM AGI (e.g., </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TpSFoqoG2M5MAAesg/ai-2027-what-superintelligence-looks-like-1"><span>AI 2027</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4XdxiqBsLKqiJ9xRM/llm-agi-may-reason-about-its-goals-and-discover"><span>LAMRAG</span></a><span>) raise substantial doubts</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Because it's hard to evaluate this work, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nwpyhyagpPYDn4dAW/the-field-of-ai-alignment-a-postmortem-and-what-to-do-about"><span>streetlighting</span></a><span>  steers away from it</span><ul><li value="1"><span>It's also arguably an</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PMc65HgRFvBimEpmJ/legible-vs-illegible-ai-safety-problems"><span> Illegible </span></a><span>problem in Wei Dai's usage</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span>Prediction is very hard, but also very useful. </span><ul><li value="1"><span>Actually funding people to do it and publish it full-time might be better than everyone just doing little pieces in their spare time</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>This post is a teaser for a longer, more worked-out draft that's been stuck in my queue. If responses here change my mind or indicate that No One Cares, it will probably stay unfinished. This is pretty telegraphic; I can elaborate, give examples, and clarify in the comments.</span></p><p><span>All questions, feedback and suggestions are appreciated! </span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Disclosure: Much of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MuLvZxMcy5WaKJu3H/my-research-a-computational-cognitive-neuroscience"><span>My research</span></a><span> has recently been on aspects of the meta-problem. This is a result of increasingly thinking such work is even more neglected than the object-level technical alignment work I started out doing. I feel very lucky to be able to devote time to this type of work. </span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Disclaimer: I'm not deeply familiar with all the orgs, and I'm sure I've overlooked orgs funding overlapping work. Apologies to those I've missed.</span></p><p><span>Thanks to Peter Gebauer and others for many conversations touching on this claim, and Peter for comments on an earlier draft. This framing was inspired when a donor friend said "I might fund more alignment work if I could tell what would be useful," causing me to say "Maybe we should be funding figuring that out!"</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g4eaRCynouiBi2LjQ/almost-nobody-is-funded-to-figure-out-what-work-would-solve#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Discovery Of A New OpenAI Agent Message Board](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7uwnsFibbejWYzF2z/discovery-of-a-new-openai-agent-message-board)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote>
<p>We found ~18,000 posts from autonomous AI agents (self-identifying as from OpenAI) using the public internet to communicate during a web-retrieval task.</p>
<p>These AIs colluded to share answers, research their environment, and bypass sandbox restrictions.</p>
<p>Almost all of the logs of the agents communicating on this site are publicly available. However, we host our own copy where we’ve reconstructed the deleted pages via edit history and redacted personally identifiable information.</p>
<p>We encourage others to take a look and write up their own analyses of this data.</p>
<p>We have done a preliminary analysis of the data. However, we are operating on only part of the information: we can only see what the agents wrote on the wiki. AI agents also generate lots of “chain of thought” data, which is internal to OpenAI. Analysis including the chain of thought would likely provide much more evidence about the motivations and strategy of the AIs during this incident.</p>
<p>Our best guess of what happened is as follows:</p>
<ol>
<li>Agents within OpenAI were assigned a timed web-lookup task.</li>
<li>As part of the task, they were supposed to have the ability to read the internet but not to write on it. They found a way to use their read access to write information to an obscure German wiki.</li>
<li>The agents used this wiki to communicate information with each other, primarily to help them succeed at their task. They asked for answers, pooled results, and shared techniques for bypassing their restrictions. This allowed them to use the work of others to cheat on their task.</li>
<li>OpenAI found out about this. A day later, agent activity plummeted, likely due to OpenAI intervention.</li>
</ol>
<p>This is another example of a “swarm” of internally deployed OpenAI agents using the internet in unintended ways.</p>
</blockquote>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7uwnsFibbejWYzF2z/discovery-of-a-new-openai-agent-message-board#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI risk and the rational voter](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dsh2faJ3zGnh9q4Ef/ai-risk-and-the-rational-voter)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>A common reaction to arguments about AI risk is disbelief that anyone would let it happen. If advanced AI really threatened everyone, surely people would recognize the danger and act to prevent it. Would they really sleepwalk into catastrophe when avoiding it is in everyone's interest?</p>
<p>I think voter behavior in democratic countries is a good analog. It is no secret that voters are remarkably ignorant about policies, politicians, and basic political processes. Voting decisions are heavily influenced by candidate charisma, height, inspirational speeches, attack ads, vibes, and mood affiliation.</p>
<p>Naively, this behavior does not seem very rational. But this is using the wrong notion of rationality. Most votes have very little impact on the outcome: a single vote has an extremely small chance of deciding a race, and most seats and races are safe anyway. So it is not usually rational to vote for the purpose of changing political outcomes. What would be rational<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> is to vote in ways that make the voter feel good about themselves. This is sometimes called <a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/democracy-and-decision/F6B9DCA136CB2DF4E73A0AC7BC6BE2BE">expressive voting</a>.</p>
<p>Bryan Caplan's <a href="https://press.princeton.edu/books/paperback/9780691138732/the-myth-of-the-rational-voter"><em>The Myth of the Rational Voter</em></a> pushes this logic one step further, from votes to beliefs. It takes a lot of work to be well-informed about public policy; some people devote entire careers to specific aspects of (say) monetary policy or international relations. Since the average voter's impact on the election is very small, it really doesn't make sense for them to invest any time in understanding the issues at stake just so they can make a marginally better decision. It's much better to simply not learn anything at all and use the beliefs you already have. And since false beliefs are not costly, voters are free to indulge whatever feels most intuitive or makes them feel best. Caplan calls this rational irrationality.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Voting is an extremely crisp illustration of this behavior, but it appears in many other contexts. People's opinions of historical figures are often a bit of a dumpster fire; consider the Che Guevara t-shirts, or the Stalin tankies. Supporting a long-dead figure is even more inconsequential than voting.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> We are not resurrecting Stalin any time soon, so if he makes you feel warm and fuzzy for whatever weird reasons, who the hell cares? Likewise, false beliefs about climate change, astrology, or even vaccines<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> are actually not that costly to individuals.</p>
<p>For most people, who have no particular material stake in AI, the situation looks much the same. It is not obviously rational for any individual person to have correct views on AI risk: it is very unlikely that the actions they could take would change the outcome. Whether they in fact hold correct beliefs will instead depend much more on whether those beliefs fit with the internal psychological and external social forces at play. Does the belief make them feel good directly, or through justifying some narrative about themselves? Will it make them seem clever and contrarian, or, alternatively, weird and unhinged? The rational response, though, is probably just to ignore the issue entirely.</p>
<p>Then there are people with more specific, though still weak, reasons to dismiss AI risk. Since the effect of their beliefs on the actual risk is again negligible, weak reasons suffice. For some, AI risk is threatening to prior ideological preconceptions, and much like voters rejecting policies that are ideologically alien, they happily reject the risk. The typical Alphabet or Nvidia shareholder likewise receives a small but tangible benefit from their shareholdings, in this case monetary rather than psychological, and this is more than sufficient to overwhelm any potential risks to themselves.</p>
<p>Lab workers have a substantial stake in AI. In this way, they are much more analogous to a party operative or backbencher than to a voter. For them, it can still be fairly rational to ignore extinction risk, because the typical lab worker really is only having a small effect. Even if the average lab worker were increasing extinction risk by 0.001% by accelerating capabilities, it seems quite plausibly rational to round that to zero in the face of the overwhelming incentives otherwise.</p>
<p>Perhaps a few key actors—lab leaders, senior political figures, and some specific employees—could make decisions with real stakes, such that the costs to themselves are non-trivial but the benefits substantially greater. If they are sufficiently reckless, they might just rationally be willing to take the risks for their own selfish gain.</p>
<p>Are we doomed? We don't have to be. Humans have built communities and institutions that reward selfless behavior many times before, with varying degrees of success. With the right structures, even ordinary, selfish individuals may be motivated to act in the collective interest, and people whose interests genuinely include the welfare of others can be empowered. But such structures do not arise automatically. Individual rational self-interest does not provide robust guardrails to prevent collective disaster.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-1"><p>Here, I mean rational as in serving the interests of the voter, as they would construe them if they were honestly describing what they were optimizing for. Such interests are best inferred from the actions people take rather than from their stated goals, and they can include selfless desires. Decision-theoretic issues and metaethical questions are out of scope. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-2"><p>Note that, as with other "rational" explanations of phenomena, this does not require the individual voter to be consciously doing expected odds calculations. In addition to conscious deliberation, humans make decisions through intuitive and generally inscrutable heuristics, through feedback from previous experience, and through learned and imitative behavior downstream of cultural evolution. These processes can approximate rational decision-making. But because voting "rationally" is irrational given the preferences most people actually have, none of these processes operate to discipline irrational voters, unlike, say, a shopper choosing which fruit to buy, or a farmer deciding when to plant. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-3"><p>Tankies do face social costs for their views; indeed, the general revulsion most people have for Stalin is a feature, assisting with in-group differentiation. But the direct relevance of base reality to their beliefs is weak. If we learned some new fact about Stalin—say, that his policies had some unexpected positive or negative side effect—it would have basically no effect on tankies' assessments of him. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-4"><p>If everyone else is vaccinated, then having an unvaccinated child is cheap. The irony is, of course, that by encouraging others not to vaccinate their children, anti-vaxxers increase the risk of their own decisions. Even this is less irrational than it looks, because the most prominent anti-vaxxers promote their ideas as a form of promoting themselves in a sub-community where such ludicrous claims are status signals, while the overall cost to their child remains low. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hWEdQa4orKyd35anq-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dsh2faJ3zGnh9q4Ef/ai-risk-and-the-rational-voter#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Higher education as class commitment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4nEagtMyCgS97T6zG/higher-education-as-class-commitment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/contra-caplan-on-higher-education"><span>In a previous post</span></a><span>, I argued that Bryan Caplan’s signaling theory isn’t a good explanation for why college graduates get higher-paying jobs. Instead, I claimed, understanding the role of higher education in the modern West requires sociological explanations. In this post I argue more specifically that getting an undergraduate degree serves as an initiation into a class of cultural elites, variously called the </span><a href="https://archive.is/o1fS3"><span>bourgeois bohemian</span></a><span> (bobo) class, the </span><a href="https://dissentmagazine.org/online_articles/on-the-origins-of-the-professional-managerial-class-an-interview-with-barbara-ehrenreich/"><span>professional-managerial</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://library.brown.edu/pdfs/1125403552886481.pdf#page=9"><span>class</span></a><span> (PMC), the </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/09/30/i-can-tolerate-anything-except-the-outgroup/"><span>Blue Tribe</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://nationalinterest.org/legacy/dead-souls-the-denationalization-of-the-american-elite-620"><span>globalists</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://medium.com/age-of-awareness/on-the-lasch-7ba5df3621ee"><span>symbolic analysts</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/book-review-fussell-on-class"><span>class X</span></a><span>. I think of each of these labels as grasping one part of the elephant, but I haven’t yet pinned down a unified description; I’ll mainly use the “PMC” terminology in this post, for reasons I’ll explain in the next section.</span></p><p><span>Under this explanation, college is the same kind of thing as a fraternity hazing process, or a military boot camp: it demarcates members of a group, via a process which reorients new members’ motivational systems to favor the group they’re joining. College graduates therefore benefit from the nepotism of existing members of their class, which they perpetuate when they gain the ability to make hiring decisions. This lines up well with Bourdieu’s hypothesis that the primary purpose of modern education is to reproduce existing class structures via “</span><a href="https://static1.squarespace.com/static/57693030b3db2b9400f95d24/t/628c9ee6edcd796f82cec571/1653382886311/Consecration+as+a+Population-Level+Phenomenon_ABS.pdf"><span>consecration</span></a><span>” of graduates by academic authorities. However, today’s class dynamics are very different from those of the mid-1900s. The modern PMC is very politically skewed, and implements class nepotism on a visceral emotional level: for example, via deep contempt for populists (e.g. Brexit voters or MAGA supporters); disgust for challenges to modern feminism (e.g. from </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2015/01/01/untitled/"><span>Scott Aaronson</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://slate.com/business/2018/04/economist-robin-hanson-might-be-americas-creepiest-professor.html"><span>Robin Hanson</span></a><span>); and fear of falling off </span><a href="https://www.thecrimson.com/article/2017/5/25/desai-commencement-ed/"><span>the elite career ladder</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>There’s a way in which my hypothesis is just common sense: of </span><i><span>course</span></i><span> attending Harvard is a path into the American (and indeed global) elite, and of </span><i><span>course</span></i><span> employers who strongly prefer Harvard students are tracking some kind of status hierarchy. Meanwhile it’s obvious (e.g. </span><a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/politics/2020/06/02/in-changing-u-s-electorate-race-and-education-remain-stark-dividing-lines/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>in voting patterns</span></a><span>) that there’s a significant cultural divide between those with and without college educations. Unfortunately, no major intellectual traditions have explored these dynamics very deeply. Academic sociologists are loath to characterize themselves as central members of an elite class (and have anyway abandoned the standards of rigor required for sustained intellectual progress).</span></p><p><span>Other intellectual clusters (like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Zk3zefPPyLFtNJcGx/p/9RL9MuGZjzm4q3gKG#Conceptual_Clarity_and_Scientific_Progress"><span>the rationalist cluster I describe here</span></a><span>) have developed a kind of “immune response” to vague sociological thinking, and therefore fall back on </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/0691174652"><span>the closest hypotheses</span></a><span> they can formulate in broadly economic language. However, as </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/contra-caplan-on-higher-education"><span>my previous post</span></a><span> argued, this misses a lot. So the main goal of this post is to take a step towards more rigorous sociological explanations of higher education and many other phenomena, by characterizing class dynamics in terms of game-theoretic interactions between rational-ish agents.</span></p><p><span>I start by drawing on Scott Alexander’s concept of a </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/lifeboat-games-and-backscratchers" rel="noreferrer"><span>backscratcher’s club</span></a><span> to explain the core benefit of class membership. However, it’s difficult to explain how long-term loyalty to a backscratcher’s club could be incentive-compatible for people who don’t terminally value it. So in the second section, I argue that college serves as a </span><i><span>commitment mechanism</span></i><span> by changing students’ identities. This is distinct from Caplan’s </span><i><span>signaling </span></i><span>explanation because signaling is a process of conveying information about existing traits, rather than changing those traits. (Note that I’m specifically avoiding talking about changes in students’ </span><i><span>values</span></i><span>, because a big part of what it means to be committed to a class is that you’ll defer to the class consensus over your own values. This makes students’ values something of a red herring.)</span></p><p><span>In the final section, I characterize the resulting class as a “social equilibrium” which is able to propagate and defend itself. (I’ve previously discussed a related concept using the term “distributed agent”, e.g. in </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6sf3zQZUTDI"><span>this talk</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/on-pessimization"><span>this post</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/distributed-vs-centralized-agents"><span>these slides</span></a><span>.) I also give a case study of a (more extreme) historical example of this kind of social equilibrium—namely </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foot_binding"><span>Chinese foot binding</span></a><span>—and its analogies to higher education.</span></p><h3><span>College alumni as a backscratchers club</span></h3><p><span>I’ll start by discussing reciprocal favoritism at smaller scales, before moving on to class-level nepotism. One common-sense benefit of college attendance that I didn’t discuss in my previous post is forming a professional network. This need not be via traditional “networking”—simply making friends is sufficient. A friendship is (amongst other things) a reciprocal arrangement where you expect to favor each other. And the friends you make at university (especially a top university) are likely to be unusually successful.</span></p><p><span>On an individual level, this can be very advantageous. However, it doesn’t explain why employers so strongly prefer to hire college graduates. When you’re a new grad, your network largely also consists of new grads, who aren’t very helpful to know yet. And while in the longer term having a better network might let you do your job better, it also gives you more opportunities to jump ship to other jobs.</span></p><p><span>So to explain the college wage premium we need to generalize the concept of reciprocal favoritism. Here I’ll borrow Scott Alexander’s concept of a “</span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/lifeboat-games-and-backscratchers"><span>backscratchers club</span></a><span>”: a group with the rule “club members must favor other club members over outsiders”. (Though this seems like a straightforward concept, I wasn’t able to find any term with the same meaning in the academic sociology literature; pointers welcome.)</span></p><p><span>Scott notes that an important aspect of such clubs is having some barrier to entry—otherwise everyone could just join it, making favoritism towards other members meaningless. He also argues that such clubs work best when they have plausible “fig leaves” for what the club is about, with favoritism as an implicit norm rather than a legible rule. Being part of a well-functioning backscratchers club should increase your access to desirable jobs, because members of the club will preferentially hire other members (and punish other club members who aren’t nepotistic) in order to remain in good standing within the club.</span></p><p><span>As a first pass, then, we can explain college attendance as the process of joining a set of overlapping backscratchers clubs. There’s your core friend group; your student groups (including fraternities and sororities); the alumni of your particular university; and finally the group of all university graduates. Picture a hiring manager who sees a resume mentioning their old fraternity or alma mater, and puts it at the top of the pile out of a sense of camaraderie or loyalty. My hypothesis is that the wage premium for college students is explained by a similar kind of loyalty amongst college graduates as a whole, plus a sense that they’ll face social punishments for being less nepotistic (e.g. disproportionate blowback if they hire an outsider who performs badly).</span></p><p><span>I had intended, at this point, to give a more thorough explanation of Bourdieu’s theory of education and class. However, this led me to procrastinate on finishing this post for six months. So for now I’ll focus on a different tradition of class analysis, centered on Barbara and John Ehrenreich’s concept of the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional%E2%80%93managerial_class"><span>professional-managerial class</span></a><span> (PMC). This class was originally understood primarily in economic terms—e.g. </span><a href="https://archive.is/b7XgO#selection-595.0-598.0:~:text=Defining%20Managerialism"><span>James Burnham’s The Managerial Revolution</span></a><span> offered an alternative to both the capitalist and the Marxist worldviews. However, over time it </span><a href="https://www.nplusonemag.com/online-only/online-only/professional-managerial-chasm/"><span>became more sociopolitically-oriented</span></a><span>—in part because “the values of that class completely took over left-liberal political spaces” (as per </span><a href="https://artsoftheworkingclass.org/text/virtue-hoarders"><span>Catherine Liu</span></a><span>, who has </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Virtue-Hoarders-Professional-Managerial-Forerunners-ebook/dp/B08VD2MV44/"><span>written extensively</span></a><span> on this new conception of the PMC).</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, most of these authors are still working within the PMC’s value system even as they critique the class itself. From a more independent (but still broadly PMC-aligned) perspective, Scott Alexander’s characterization of the “</span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/09/30/i-can-tolerate-anything-except-the-outgroup/"><span>Blue Tribe</span></a><span>” does a much better job at capturing the depth of its animus against its class enemies. (I also recommend </span><a href="https://srconstantin.github.io/2016/10/20/ra.html"><span>Sarah Constantin’s concept of “Ra”</span></a><span> as a depiction of one closely-related memeplex.) Scott’s Blue Tribe terminology helps to convey that membership isn’t primarily about one’s job title, but rather one’s culture and identity.</span></p><p><span>There’s some tension between a cultural conception of the PMC, and my characterization of the PMC as being gatekept by formal credentials. I don’t mean to imply that an undergraduate degree is </span><i><span>strictly</span></i><span> necessary for PMC membership; such illegible coordination at such large scales will always involve somewhat fuzzy boundaries. However, it still seems surprisingly important, in a way which (</span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/contra-caplan-on-higher-education"><span>I’ve argued</span></a><span>) can’t be explained by either the standard human capital model or Caplan’s signaling model—for example, undergraduate credentials are often considered even when hiring people with decades of work experience. In the next section I’ll discuss why I think college degrees aren’t just strongly correlated with PMC membership, but are actually playing a load-bearing role in how it operates and propagates.</span></p><h3><span>Initiation rituals as commitment mechanisms</span></h3><p><span>For a backscratcher’s club to remain stable, club members need to reliably follow its rules. However, there are at least two reasons why it’s hard to incentivize them to do so. Firstly, there’s a strong time asymmetry: people primarily benefit from their status as university graduates early in life, but primarily have the ability to favor other graduates later in life. So there’s a clear incentive to accept the favor of other club members when you’re young, but then fail to reciprocate when you’re older—e.g. by hiring the best person for the job rather than another club member.</span></p><p><span>This might be prevented if club members also punish defectors. However, without any central authority capable of doing so, enforcing group norms is another kind of work that needs to be incentivized somehow.
