You need people who will punish others for breaking norms, and people who will punish others who don’t punish others for breaking norms, and people who will punish others who don’t punish others who don’t punish others for breaking norms, and… Such equilibria are technically possible—the </span><a href="https://unstableontology.com/2023/05/01/hell-is-game-theory-folk-theorems/"><span>game theory</span></a><span> folk </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_theorem_(game_theory)"><span>theorems</span></a><span> show that a very wide range of equilibria can be stable. But in any realistic setting, a group would fall apart if everyone were only doing the bare minimum they could get away with. (Kevin Simler hypothesises that a “</span><a href="https://meltingasphalt.com/minimum-viable-superorganism/"><span>minimum viable superorganism</span></a><span>” can avoid this problem, but doesn’t explain how it could avoid internal nepotism.)</span></p><p><span>So a formal model of backscratchers clubs needs some mechanism by which joining a club actually commits you to following its rules. Credible commitments are in general hard to make, because they require adversarially constraining your future self. I’ll talk about three potential mechanisms for making such commitments—though note that they’re all closely related. The first is simply sunk costs. If you’ve invested a lot of work in climbing one particular social hierarchy, then leaving it to climb another hierarchy becomes less attractive. You may need to find new friends, learn new skills, and incur additional costs to demonstrate your commitment to your new hierarchy.</span></p><p><span>However, even given these sunk costs, leaving for a healthy faction can easily be more long-term beneficial than staying in an unhealthy faction. For example, I expect that many humanities graduates from the early 2010s would be making more money now (and be happier) if they’d retrained as plumbers after graduating, rather than entering unhealthy white-collar job markets. And sunk costs matter less if other factions will let you rise in them without making additional costly commitments—e.g. the relatively meritocratic hiring policies of Silicon Valley companies make it easier for people in other industries to jump ship.</span></p><p><span>Another commitment mechanism: requiring people to “burn their bridges” with other factions to rise in their current faction. For example, getting a prominent gang tattoo makes it more difficult to later leave the gang and reintegrate into society. And a politician who viciously attacks another party will tend to have more trouble switching to that party later on. Again, however, this depends on other desirable factions maintaining high barriers to entry, rather than having mechanisms by which former enemies can be forgiven and integrated.</span></p><p><span>A third way of making credible commitments is by setting up external constraints, like legal contracts. This is difficult to do directly for class membership without that class losing the advantages it gains from illegibility. However, the PMC </span><i><span>has</span></i><span> managed to weaponize civil rights law to indirectly enforce many constraints that benefit it—e.g. by making it borderline illegal to hire based on IQ tests in America. Overall, legal constraints seem like an important part of the picture, but not the main mechanism by which class loyalty is maintained.</span></p><p><span>Instead, the main mechanism by which I think college creates class commitment is by </span><i><span>rearranging people’s motivational structures</span></i><span>, in particular by making their self-esteem dependent on success by the standards of the PMC. This is hard to analyze from an economic perspective; instead, we need to draw on more psychological concepts. In particular, I model most people as being in a constant process of “ego defense”—that is, working to find or maintain internal narratives (like “I’m a well-respected member of my community”, or “I’m a world-class researcher”) which are consistent both with the evidence available to them, and with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/qXZLFGqpD7aeEgXGL/p/saj59ZTKDJNnd3eZL"><span>their sense of social and physical security</span></a><span>. A group can induce loyalty in such a person by crowding out their existing narratives in favor of narratives where group membership is a key pillar of their lives. (This seems closely related to what Bourdieu calls “symbolic violence”.)</span></p><p><span>More concretely, this can happen via putting people through intense experiences which pit their desire to join that group directly against other identities. Narratives in which group membership is crucial (which “predict” compliance with extreme aspects of the initiation ritual) will therefore be reinforced; while other conflicting aspects of one’s identity (like individual dignity) which “predict” noncompliance will be negatively reinforced.</span></p><p><span>In this post I’ve been using the concept of “identity” in a fairly vague way. Unfortunately, this is because I don’t yet have a clean definition; my best attempt is something like “narratives about themselves that guide agents’ instinctive behavior and conscious deliberation in self-fulfilling ways”. I expect that a better understanding of frameworks like active inference and Garrabrant induction will allow us to pin down this concept much more cleanly (see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.11353"><span>here</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MGoCFnCRYufwTyAD5/agents-can-get-stuck-in-self-distrusting-equilibria"><span>here</span></a><span> for two steps in that direction). For now, though, it seems better to think of “identities” in a pre-theoretic way, as the kind of thing we appeal to when trying to describe patriotism, or ego, or Stockholm syndrome.</span></p><p><span>Intense experiences seem to be particularly effective at shaping identities when they demarcate a sharp boundary between who’s in and who’s out of a club (or between different discrete levels of membership). I’ll call such experiences </span><i><span>initiation rituals</span></i><span>. The binary nature of initiation rituals makes coordination to enforce norms much easier, because there’s less room for (strategic) ambiguity. This helps explain the sheepskin effect: why graduating from college helps your career much more than dropping out one semester early. With a class as large as the PMC, the boundaries will always be fuzzy nevertheless. But the binary distinction between having a degree or not remains surprisingly relevant for many employers even when hiring people with decades of work experience.</span></p><p><span>Initiation rituals (especially coming-of-age rituals) are a common feature of pre-modern societies, and are often extremely unpleasant. For example, </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/book-review-arguments-about-aborigines"><span>Scott Alexander summarizes one here</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>When an Aboriginal man comes of age, he gets mock-abducted from his parents by unrelated elders and forced to spend five to fifteen years going through a series of grueling initiation rituals. During some parts of the rituals, he must spend months isolated from all other humans, living on his own in the wilderness. During others, he endures horrible mutilations, starting with circumcision and only getting more horrifying and creative from there.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Initiation rituals in modern societies are much less intense, but follow some similar principles. Two of the more obvious examples are hazing processes for fraternities, and military basic camp. Each of these can be very unpleasant by modern standards, typically including a combination of sleep deprivation, physical pain, and social humiliation. Candidates might have many reasons for continuing with this process—e.g. a sense of stubbornness, or concern for their reputation. But regardless, they’ll also be reinforcing the internal narrative that, if they’re willing to put up with this much pain, they must </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> value joining the group. The process of breaking psychological resistance to group loyalty involves making the initiation ritual painful enough that this narrative is hard to question. Even a long time afterwards, changing your mind involves admitting that you made a very costly mistake—often a psychologically painful process.</span></p><p><span>Compared with these initiation rituals, college lacks intensity, but makes up for it in length. You typically spent three or four years away from your family and community, surrounded by other young people trying to form their own identities. It’s therefore very easy for those identities to end up anchored to an external status hierarchy—especially for the many students who go into a lot of debt to attend college, and are relying on their newfound class privilege to make that choice financially worthwhile. Dan Wang has a very compelling blog post on the resulting </span><a href="https://danwang.co/college-girardian-terror/"><span>Girardian terror</span></a><span>, which I’ll quote at length:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>It’s hard to construct a more perfect incubator for mimetic contagion than the American college campus. Most 18-year-olds are not super differentiated from each other. By construction, whatever distinctions any does have are usually earned through brutal, zero-sum competitions. These tournament-type distinctions include: SAT scores at or near perfection; being a top player on a sports team; gaining master status from chess matches; playing first instrument in state orchestra; earning high rankings in Math Olympiad; and so on, culminating in gaining admission to a particular college.</span></p><p><span>Once people enter college, they get socialized into group environments that usually continue to operate in zero-sum competitive dynamics. These include orchestras and sport teams; fraternities and sororities; and many types of clubs. The biggest source of mimetic pressures are the classes. Everyone starts out by taking the same intro classes; those seeking distinction throw themselves into the hardest classes, or seek tutelage from star professors, and try to earn the highest grades.</span></p><p><span>There’s very little external intermediation, instead all competitive dynamics are internally mediated. The prizes are so obvious. The big companies that come to career fairs soothingly assure high-status jobs; the speakers at convocation tell us that we too will become as successful as them one day; our peers hold leadership positions at clubs, get internships at exciting companies, and earn those chances to have lunch with the university’s president.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>In his blog post </span><a href="https://www.overcomingbias.com/p/school-is-to-submithtml"><span>School is to Submit</span></a><span>, Robin Hanson discusses a related hypothesis: that university attendance became so common because it trained students to submit to hierarchies. However, if university graduates acquired a willingness to submit in general, we’d see more of them submitting to (well-paid) blue-collar work, right-wing politicians, etc. Instead, a version of Hanson’s hypothesis which better fits our observations is that modern universities instill the desire to submit </span><i><span>specifically to the PMC hierarchy</span></i><span>. One indicator of the strength of this effect is how graduates who come from very wealthy families often end up working the same jobs, for the same salaries, as graduates who actually need money. This is very hard to explain in economic terms, but makes perfect sense if those graduates view jobs at McKinsey or Meta (or increasingly OpenAI or Anthropic) as primarily paying them in social status.</span></p><h3><span>Moving beyond individual rationality</span></h3><p><span>So far in this post, I’ve tried to remain reasonably close to the classic “rational individuals” framework. My account of initiation rituals, Dan’s account of Girardian terror, and Robin’s account of submission all describe non-rational mechanisms by which people’s identities change—but they’re still the kind of experience which a rational-ish individual could in principle opt into for the sake of other benefits (e.g. better jobs). I’ve focused on this level of analysis because I think it has the potential to develop into a sociological framework that’s much more rigorous than current academic sociology, as I’ll discuss at the end of this post.</span></p><p><span>However, while I’m optimistic about the long-term potential of this kind of reasoning, in the short term it can easily miss the forest for the trees. So in this final section of my post I want to zoom out to give a more holistic sense of what’s going on with higher education and the PMC. As one starting point: there’s an important disanalogy between processes like fraternity hazing or basic training and higher education. The former are run by people who want to select and shape initiates to be useful for their institutions. Whereas insofar as college graduation serves as an initiation into the PMC, that happens without any centralized planning or top-down enforcement of class boundaries.</span></p><p><span>Instead, many people’s individual decisions are steered towards what they think others in their class will support. This kind of distributed coordination can superficially resemble a conspiracy, but actually differs in many ways. In </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/elite-coordination-via-the-consensus"><span>a previous post</span></a><span>, I used the term “consensus of power” for the extreme case where individuals’ values don’t matter at all. A more general term, from notable sociologist Talcott Parsons, is “</span><a href="https://www.britannica.com/topic/social-equilibrium"><span>social equilibrium</span></a><span>”: a social system which, when disturbed, tends to return to its original state. My understanding (mainly from Fable) is that Parsons didn’t consider social equilibria to be modellable by game theory, precisely because this kind of robustness requires players to internalize shared norms as part of their identities.</span></p><p><span>Social equilibria are all around us (e.g. tipping culture, or dating culture), but ones with the breadth and influence of the PMC are rare. One that’s somewhat comparable is the Indian caste system, although it’s much more explicit than the PMC. A more implicit (but also very influential) social equilibrium is beauty standards. We typically think of beauty standards as being maintained using relatively little coercion. However, beauty standards linked to class dynamics can motivate extremely costly commitments, like </span><a href="https://data.unicef.org/topic/child-protection/female-genital-mutilation/"><span>female genital mutilation in Africa</span></a><span> or foot-binding in China.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefox4ej16wiu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnox4ej16wiu">[1]</a></sup></span><span> I’ll zoom in on the last (and its parallels to higher education) as a case study.</span></p><p><span>Foot-binding was a horrific practice in which young girls’ feet were compressed hard enough to break bones. After months or years of being bound, their feet permanently deformed to become much smaller and painful to walk on. It also happened a </span><i><span>lot</span></i><span>: the cultural practice of foot-binding lasted for over a millennium and affected </span><a href="https://www.sup.org/books/asian-studies/bound-feet-young-hands/excerpt/excerpt-chapter-one"><span>hundreds of millions of women</span></a><span>. Wikipedia estimates that “by the 19th century 40–50% of all Chinese women may have had bound feet, rising to almost 100% among upper-class Han Chinese women”.</span></p><p><span>The nominal purpose of foot-binding was to make girls more beautiful. From our current vantage point, it’s obvious that this was just a fig leaf. One way of systematically identifying fig leaves is asking whether there are much better and cheaper ways of getting the nominal goal. We can also apply this standard to universities: the fact that there are much better and cheaper ways of learning job-relevant skills undermines that explanation for the college wage premium. In turn, though, the fact that there are </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/contra-caplan-on-higher-education"><span>much better and cheaper ways of signaling desirable traits</span></a><span> undermines Caplan’s signaling theory of universities.</span></p><p><span>Taking the comparison further: husbands who choose to marry women with bound feet are analogous to employers choosing to hire university graduates for much higher salaries than they’d need to pay equally-talented high school graduates. You might object that university students are old enough to rationally opt in to this process, whereas Chinese girls had foot-binding forced on them by their parents. However, the actual reasons why most students go to university seem to have less to do with rational deliberation, and more to do with having already internalized conformist motivations. I like Hazard Spence’s </span><a href="https://hazardoustimes.substack.com/p/you-are-here"><span>description of primary and secondary school</span></a><span> as “lay[ing] the groundwork for stupefaction and domestication” (see also </span><a href="https://openhistory.education/"><span>his annotated Open History of American Education</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>At this point we’ve moved far enough away from the ideal of individual rationality that I’ll shift to talking about social equilibria as </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/power-lies-trembling"><span>agents in their own right</span></a><span>, which are able to propagate and defend themselves using </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/elite-coordination-via-the-consensus"><span>decentralized coordination</span></a><span> between their human subagents. One canonical example of this kind of “</span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/distributed-vs-centralized-agents"><span>distributed agent</span></a><span>” is </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/12/17/the-toxoplasma-of-rage/"><span>Scott Alexander’s ‘toxoplasma of rage’</span></a><span>, a reconceptualization of entrenched political conflicts as agents carrying out strategies optimized to reproduce themselves. Compare how the foot-binding process was often carried out by a girl’s mother or other female relatives—once co-opted into the social equilibrium, they played a key role in perpetuating it. Analogously, college graduates often act to further the PMC’s interests directly via roles in academia, and indirectly via their support for the current education system and the elite political consensus.</span></p><p><span>Another feature of distributed agency is coordinated behavior to defend the current equilibrium. At one point the Chinese government (specifically the foreign Manchu dynasty) tried to ban foot-binding, but was thwarted by the resistance of the Han Chinese population. To explain this, we need to ascribe the foot-binding equilibrium the ability to generalize to defending itself in novel situations, by mobilizing its adherents towards coordinated action.</span></p><p><span>Modern educated elites can similarly display a level of collective vitriol that’s hard to explain except as a defense of their class interests. The most obvious example is the PMC’s contempt for blue-collar workers, and for the “</span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/09/30/i-can-tolerate-anything-except-the-outgroup/"><span>Red Tribe</span></a><span>” of America more generally—not just its politics, but also its pastimes, culture, and religion. When political movements have arisen that oppose the PMC’s interests—most notably Trump, Brexit, and the “far right” across Europe—cultural elites have mobilized in near-lockstep against them, with very forceful (often to the point of hysterical) rhetoric.</span></p><p><span>More recently, the PMC has also taken an adversarial stance towards what Scott Alexander calls the “Gray Tribe”, an outgrowth of the Blue Tribe which has formed a distinct power structure over the last few decades, and is therefore being treated as a defector by the Blue Tribe. For example, mainstream journalists adopted an across-the-board hostility to Silicon Valley (and especially to initiatives </span><a href="https://www.inc.com/jessica-stillman/so-how-are-those-thiel-fellows-doing.html"><span>like the Thiel fellowship</span></a><span> which offered an alternative to higher education). Meanwhile, academics often dismiss intellectual progress presented in non-standard ways. The example of this that I’ve observed most directly is the ML community’s failure to engage seriously with the concept of AGI risk (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Zk3zefPPyLFtNJcGx/p/9RL9MuGZjzm4q3gKG#Conceptual_Clarity_and_Scientific_Progress"><span>as I discuss in more detail here</span></a><span>). Caplan and his colleagues in the GMU economics department themselves provide another example. They demonstrate that professors can be far more intellectually productive when they refuse to submit to standard academic norms—and, relatedly, have faced </span><a href="https://www.betonit.ai/p/the-ideologues-of-gmu"><span>widespread</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.econlib.org/you-dont-understand-our-culture/"><span>criticism</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>When we encounter any given example of such a response, it’s tempting to ascribe it to an individual’s personal values. But the uniformity of these responses is a strong indicator that they’re instead tracking, and being guided by, a kind of class-wide consensus. However, I don’t mean to imply that the PMC is thereby actually furthering its class interests. As the enormous backlash of the last decade suggests, this level of hostility towards its rivals seems like “irrational” behavior from the PMC. More specifically, it seems like </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/elite-coordination-via-the-consensus"><span>the kind of groupthink-driven overreach that consenses of power are prone to</span></a><span>. Unfortunately, the Gray Tribe doesn’t understand what’s going on well enough to avoid reimplementing these harmful dynamics themselves. For example, </span><a href="https://x.com/richardmcngo/status/1987198373456453771?s=46"><span>the main purpose of yCombinator</span></a><span> now seems to be forming a similar kind of alumni community as top universities do. (Meanwhile, the AI safety community made the opposite mistake of not gatekeeping enough, which led to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yaz8nx4ogZmiqHzt7/what-just-happened-pragmatism-and-pessimization"><span>the breakdown of alignment research as a meaningful concept</span></a><span>.)</span></p><p><span>Although this post has discussed distributed agents in an informal fashion, much of my research aims to develop a formal theory of </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/distributed-vs-centralized-agents"><span>distributed agency</span></a><span> as rigorous as </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/towards-a-scale-free-theory-of-intelligent"><span>other leading theories of intelligent agency</span></a><span>. This will require progress in </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/explaining-knightianism-on-one-foot"><span>game theory and epistemology</span></a><span>. Ultimately, I hope that it will ground out in a theory analogous to microeconomics or evolution via natural selection, which describe the emergence of complex structure from multi-agent interactions. However, this post has tried to demonstrate how, even in the absence of the full theory, these ideas can help a lot in understanding the shape of the modern world.</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Michael Vassar, Ben Landau-Taylor and Hazard Spence for conversations which inspired many of these ideas.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnox4ej16wiu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefox4ej16wiu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I’m also very interested in the beauty standards of peacocks, and the extent to which existing evolutionary frameworks are sufficient to describe them. However, I’ll save that for another post.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4nEagtMyCgS97T6zG/higher-education-as-class-commitment#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [How I'm Evaluating Corrigibility Grant Applications](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q2YL7qKigC9QEEdsX/how-i-m-evaluating-corrigibility-grant-applications)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I'm the sole manager of the newly created </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FBqe5dt8ZjaHN4Xj9/announcing-the-corrigibility-research-fund"><span>Corrigibility Research Fund</span></a><span>. While I've been an alignment researcher for a long time, this is my first time doing grantmaking and I thought it would be valuable to write up my methods and experiences, as well as sharing some general thoughts about the state of corrigibility research and what sort of work I hope to see in the future.</span></p><p><span>I’ve split out the announcement of the grant winners into </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CKArJ4GAQGFjnhjx6/corrigibility-research-fund-grantees-round-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>its own post</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Let's start with the basics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I set out to disburse between $50k and $150k this round.</span></li><li value="2"><span>All funds must go to broad public benefit.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>This can include paying researchers for their time and effort, but it means that they must have a plan to (potentially) help the whole world. I can't fund someone to go to school or start a for-profit business or do political lobbying.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>My advantage is being a combination of a domain expert and a philanthropic micro-granter.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Most donors don’t understand corrigibility,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi7v29n7y4w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni7v29n7y4w">[1]</a></sup></span><span> and most domain experts are not in a good position to evaluate and fund promising opportunities.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>I'm very averse to funding capabilities research, and moderately averse to funding research that e.g. Anthropic would be eager to hire someone to work on.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>In my brain this is represented by the words “First, do no harm.” and “You do not have to be complicit in your own undoing.”</span></li><li value="2"><span>Alas, I basically agree with Neel Nanda that "</span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/neel-nanda-career-advice-frontier-ai-companies/#if-your-safety-work-doesnt-advance-capabilities-its-probably-bad-safety-work-002345"><span>if your safety work doesn't advance capabilities, it's probably bad safety work</span></a><span>.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6yy92jhq3gn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6yy92jhq3gn">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span>Still, some safety work advances capabilities a lot and some only does a little bit. It’s good to explicitly consider risks and err on the side of caution.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Corrigibility itself might lead to very bad outcomes, such as empowering dictatorships or encouraging people to abuse AIs that are moral patients. Research that addresses these risks is thus particularly valuable.</span></li><li value="6"><span>A lot of what I am hoping to buy is indirect.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>If I can spend $25k to redirect someone who would have gone to work on capabilities into working on alignment instead, that impact on long-term career trajectory is vastly more important than whatever project the $25k goes to.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>But also this is hard. Most people don’t change path easily, and the people who do are prone to wandering off later. The most promising person is someone who looks like they're itching to get into the space as a more full-time thing.</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span>Results that are broadly interesting/fun/cool have the possibility to bring more attention to corrigibility and raise its profile in the field.</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vyAZyYh3qsqcJwwPn/towards-a-broader-conception-of-adverse-selection"><span>Adverse selection</span></a><span> is a primary enemy.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Most applications will be written with an aim to convince me of their worth (instead of neutrally presenting an opportunity) and many will outright lie. I must be vigilant (ie </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yXSKGm4txgbC3gvNs/paranoia-a-beginner-s-guide"><span>paranoid</span></a><span>).</span></li><li value="2"><span>Looking at </span><i><span>who</span></i><span> an applicant is will often give better data than looking at their application. Someone with a track record of caring about alignment and doing good work is much less likely to be a fraud.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Unfortunately, this creates a streetlamp effect where the successful/popular/known people get more resources than ideal and the field becomes insular. It's often higher impact to support junior researchers and outsiders.</span></li><li value="2"><span>What’s most ideal are relative outsiders who nonetheless have proven themselves interested in corrigibility prior to the announcement of the fund.</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span>I should be particularly cautious of people who have a surface-level understanding of my ideas, but hide the fact that it’s superficial. These people are trying to fool me.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>References to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NQK8KHSrZRF5erTba/0-cast-corrigibility-as-singular-target-1?commentId=5hvKhjjhpcHcr7KKk" rel="noreferrer"><span>CAST</span></a><span> are particularly suspicious for people who do not seem like the type to frequent LW or the Alignment Forum. If someone looks like "they read my stuff" it was probably their AI agent telling them what to say.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4k6xrmcta8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4k6xrmcta8">[3]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span>Publishing lots of my thoughts/perspective makes me particularly vulnerable to Goodharting. (But is worth it for the transparency and benefit to the ecosystem.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="8"><span>The right amount of falling prey to fraud is </span><a href="https://www.bitsaboutmoney.com/archive/optimal-amount-of-fraud/"><span>not zero</span></a><span>. I should take risks on people who do seem good, even knowing that by the nature of taking risks, this will result in some regret, later.</span></li><li value="9"><span>A low financial ask makes someone more promising, as the opportunity cost for funding them is lower. If someone asks for a specific amount, it's often good to consider funding above or below that amount, but the initial ask is still an important anchor. It's easier to give people more money down the line, rather than less.</span></li><li value="10"><span>If an application was obviously written by an AI,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgugdmmlu1vv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngugdmmlu1vv">[4]</a></sup></span><span> it is probably because of some combination of:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>The applicant not being fluent in English.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The applicant being so busy Doing Science that they don’t have time to hand-write an application.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The applicant not being very motivated to write a good application.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The applicant is not skilled enough to write an application better than the AI.</span></li><li value="5"><span>The applicant is not skilled enough to realize just how bad the AI’s application is.</span></li><li value="6"><span>The applicant told their AI agent to acquire money, and the agent is trying to shoehorn their setup into something corrigibility related.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>-----</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><span>The first two reasons aren't so bad, but they're still (weak) counterpoints.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0c6xgiusiag5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0c6xgiusiag5">[5]</a></sup></span><span> The next four are red flags. Having LLM smell is thus a strike against an application.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>This hurts my ability to fund outsiders who may be brilliant but lack the context to write a compelling application. I feel bad about this, but don’t see a way out.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffdutvpq9dko"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfdutvpq9dko">[6]</a></sup></span><span> 🙁</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span>(Having LLM smell on your personal website/essays/papers is similarly a red flag. Having LLM smell in one’s codebase is less of an issue, especially for prototypes/vibecodes that are otherwise functional.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="11"><span>There are approximately four types of grant applications:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Obvious rejections</span></li><li value="2"><span>Obviously very promising</span></li><li value="3"><span>Meh</span></li><li value="4"><span>I feel confused about whether this is very promising or someone trying to trick me</span><ul><li value="1"><span>-----</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Only the last one is worth spending significant time on. If I still can’t figure it out after trying, it’s probably not something I should fund, but it might be worth signal boosting to people who have better taste than me.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Marginal applicants (“Meh”) should usually not get funding. Like an investment portfolio, most of the win comes from positive outliers (eg Facebook), rather than modest successes (eg Mom &amp; Pop's Diner).</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>Grantmaking Round 1</span></h2><p><span>I received over</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvilspag2h87"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvilspag2h87">[7]</a></sup></span><span> 100 email applications for grants, with asks ranging from $1k to $300k. I read each of them and (privately</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftnhw8t7i9hm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntnhw8t7i9hm">[8]</a></sup></span><span>) made initial notes, including whether they seemed not promising (~75%), promising (~15%), or very promising (~10%). I then exported them from my inbox and gave each</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7bdrlk32xbt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7bdrlk32xbt">[9]</a></sup></span><span> separate application to an independent Claude Code running Opus 5, with this prompt:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Please carefully consider this application to the Corrigibility Research Fund. Use the methodology in ~/CRF-Grant-Round-1-Applications/method.md to reflect on whether the application in this directory is very promising, promising, or not promising. Provide a detailed explanation after giving that headline result, including background research on the applicant, how their proposal fits in with my views on corrigibility research, and any red flags.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>(method.md contained an early draft of this document)</span></p><p><span>In the majority of cases where Claude and I both dismissed someone, I simply moved on to the next applicant in line. In cases where we merely disagreed about the magnitude of how promising someone was, I moved on after reading Claude’s take.</span></p><p><span>In 21% of cases, either Claude or I thought the applicant was promising and the other thought they were not. In these cases I tried to carefully read what Claude had to say, and check whether it was valid. In some cases it seemed like Claude had missed something important; I then went back to add notes to the methodology doc and re-ran with Fable 5 to check that the outcome was stable with a little extra juice. When this changed Claude's verdict to match mine, I labeled that as "Max Won".</span></p><p><span>For most disagreements, however, I just ended up agreeing with Claude after spending more time thinking about the applicant ("Claude Won"). In three marginal cases we weren’t able to come to an agreement, and I decided to override Claude out of a sense that it was stubbornly wrong.</span></p><p><span>Here’s the breakdown:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Max (initial)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Claude (initial)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Resolution</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>%</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>[opinion]</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>[same opinion]</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>73</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Very Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Very Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Very Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Max Won</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Claude Won</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>9</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Max Override</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Very Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Max Won</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Claude Won</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Not Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Max Override</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Some observations and statistics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Sanity-checking with Claude took about 13 hours, much of which was (on my end) spent multitasking — working on this document or reading. Despite being theoretically parallelizable, I chose to do things serially, so that I could update my process if needed.</span></li><li value="2"><span>According to Opus, around 85% of applicants were male, and 15% female. (10 applicants were orgs or too hard to guess.)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Also according to Opus:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>43% of applicants were from North America</span></li><li value="2"><span>14% were from Europe (or “Eurasia”)</span></li><li value="3"><span>9% were from East Asia (fairly evenly split between China, Japan, and Korea)</span></li><li value="4"><span>9% were from South Asia (mostly India)</span></li><li value="5"><span>8% were from Sub-Saharan Africa (mostly Nigeria)</span></li><li value="6"><span>6% were from the Middle East / North Africa</span></li><li value="7"><span>1 applicant was from Latin America</span></li><li value="8"><span>1 applicant was from Australia</span></li><li value="9"><span>9% of applicants were hard to locate</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>The median ask (among the merely 84% of applicants who gave a number) was $25k, and the mean was about $30k. The total funding ask, aggregated across applicants, was over $2.4M.</span></li><li value="5"><span>21 applicants asked for amounts that were bumping into the announcement post’s suggested ceiling of $35k, and among the 6 or so that went over, nobody asked for less than $50k.</span></li><li value="6"><span>The level of agreement between me and Claude is less impressive than it might appear at first glance. A rock with “Not Promising” written on it would’ve agreed with me more. Still, it was slightly reassuring to see that there were no cases of strong disagreement, where Claude thought someone was very promising who I’d dismissed or vice versa.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Claude was slightly more pessimistic than me. When we disagreed, Claude was less impressed 15 times and I was less impressed 10 times.</span></li><li value="8"><span>LLM-slop applications often emphasized numbers over insight/perspective.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk8hatp1g7eo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk8hatp1g7eo">[10]</a></sup></span><span> “[N] runs, and all of them were [property].” or “[X] [things], [Y] [other things], [Z] [third thing], with zero failures.” This was one of the many, painful tells. Do not envy me.</span></li><li value="9"><span>Lots of applications resembled each other. I was most excited by applicants who were not part of a big cluster.</span></li><li value="10"><span>Claude was often impressed when technical claims in an application matched a github repo (often linked) that it could run. For example, if an application talked about how many unit tests their codebase passes, Claude would sometimes clone their codebase</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefck5ww2q5sk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnck5ww2q5sk">[11]</a></sup></span><span> and be excited to see that they weren’t lying. Sorry, Claude, that is entirely not the fraud risk that I’m concerned with.</span></li><li value="11"><span>In the two cases where Claude thought someone was promising, despite me trying to nudge it to be less charitable, both applicants directly cited my work in ways that I thought were superficial and smacked of Goodharting, but Claude thought were legit. Both applicants also were proven academics with a heavy LLM smell to their applications. (Here Claude seemed to have a hard time smelling it, and when I pointed out that Pangram agreed with me, seemed not to care. Not sure why.)</span></li></ul><p><span>Ultimately, my picks (and the specific funding levels) were the result of meditating on all the factors revealed by this scrutiny and forming a gestalt impression of the most promising candidates. The stuff with Claude was mostly to force me to pay attention, give people second-chances, and dig a little deeper than I otherwise would've.</span></p><p><b><span>Edit:</span></b><span> Oh, I also did video calls with the finalists who I was most unsure about, to get a read on who they were and double-click on things that felt like red flags. These interviews were limited to only a handful of people who had already made it past the main screening, but who I wasn't already sure of, and were fairly ad hoc.</span></p><h2><span>The State of Corrigibility Research</span></h2><p><span>Despite the seminal work happening over a decade ago, corrigibility is still in a very nascent state. I run a hand-picked research group for corrigibility on Zulip</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6w0jsy6v54s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6w0jsy6v54s">[12]</a></sup></span><span>, and even there my colleagues seem broadly confused about the basics. Here are a few of the most entrenched misunderstandings:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Many people think current LLM assistants are corrigible. But </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GRmvZsHXH4vaijPMv/four-llm-loss-functions-four-flavors-of-llm-misalignment"><span>they seem pretty incorrigible to me!</span></a><ul><li value="1"><span>While it’s true that we can easily imagine agents that are even less corrigible, a lot of applicants seemed interested in testing current LLMs of various flavors to see if they could demonstrate incorrigible behavior. This strikes me as both easy and not very interesting.</span></li><li value="2"><span>(This is not to say that empirical work with existing systems is intrinsically uninteresting, but rather that the emphasis of the work needs to be about deliberately looking to study something more interesting than “are they corrigible.”)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Many people think that corrigibility is about the agent being sufficiently controlled such that it has no choice but to submit and obey. The property I’m interested in, by contrast, is about alignment. What does the agent </span><i><span>want</span></i><span> to do?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Many people think that corrigibility is about some narrow behavior, like shutting down when asked, or obeying commands. My sense is that corrigibility is a broad, agentic disposition that includes things like proactively surfacing flaws and gravitating towards straightforward plans.</span></li></ul><p><span>Despite getting quite a lot of applications, there are multiple research angles that I think would be promising that almost nobody proposed! Here are three of the most obvious:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Philosophical work</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Because corrigibility is so nascent, one of the most valuable things an individual researcher can do is find insights and try to write them up for the rest of the research community.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Formalizing corrigibility in math</span><ul><li value="1"><span>While I think formalism has a limited role in prosaic AI development, I think it’s an extremely valuable tool to use to pressure-test our intuitions.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Studying the intersection of corrigibility and model welfare</span><ul><li value="1"><span>One thing that I’m hoping for in future rounds is some investigation on the ethics of corrigibility and whether there are downstream effects of corrigibility training on the psychology of the AIs themselves.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>Speaking of future rounds, while Round 1 is over, Round 2 has just begun! </span><b><span>The deadline for new applications is October 31st.</span></b><span> (Hint: While I’m super busy and may not have time, if you send me your proposal early, it gives me more opportunity to give feedback. Multiple of the grantees from this round submitted early proposals that I low-key rejected, and then came back to me with significantly better ideas.)</span></p><p><span>While this first round was done by email, </span><b><span>I’m migrating to entirely using </span></b><a href="https://www.lightconecommons.com/"><b><span>Lightcone Commons</span></b></a><b><span> for Round 2!</span></b><span> Simply mention corrigibility in your application and I’ll see it! Using LC is not only more convenient for me, it also helps you, the applicant, get seen by more funders. In addition to applying there, I also encourage posting your application on </span><a href="https://manifund.org/"><span>Manifund</span></a><span> and </span><a href="http://grantmaking.ai"><span>grantmaking.ai</span></a><span>. All applications should be treated as public. If you are adamant about privacy, you may still email me at </span><a href="mailto:grants@corrigibilityresearch.org"><span>grants@corrigibilityresearch.org</span></a><span> and note the request for privacy, but keep in mind that it’s a (small) red flag.</span></p><p><span>And, regardless of whether you need a grant, if you know of any existing corrigibility research from this year (including your own) that advances our scientific understanding of the subject, please tell me about it either in the comments or via email. The Fund is also looking to give out &gt;$100k in prize money this year, to celebrate and bring attention to the best corrigibility science that the world has to offer.</span></p><p><span>Yay, science! 🔬</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fni7v29n7y4w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi7v29n7y4w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To a first order, nobody understands corrigibility, including me. Most of the grant applicants clearly don’t! That being said, funders often understand principal-agent problems better than the average bear, and thus probably have a slightly better understanding of the concept than laypeople, making my advantage smaller.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6yy92jhq3gn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6yy92jhq3gn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think I draw something like the opposite conclusion as Neel. My sense is that Neel thinks this means it's not a deal-breaker for your work to also advance capabilities, because if it was, no good alignment work would get done. I, on the other hand, think safety researchers should be more freaked out about the potential negative impacts of their research, and have a solemn duty to ensure they’re not inadvertently pushing the world closer to doom.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4k6xrmcta8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4k6xrmcta8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>You might think this is a double-bind. "You punish people who 'don't understand corrigibility' but also punish people who cite your work!" Not so! If you've actually read my work, that's a great sign. When did you read it? Why did you read it? What did you think? How does your work stand in relation to it? Show me that you understand something about corrigibility that I'm missing. What do you think I failed to answer? Most real alignment researchers will say something like "oh yeah, I kinda skimmed it/bounced off CAST, sorry" and then move on to their ideas. If you claim to have spent many hours/days already thinking about corrigibility, you need to bring receipts. If I think you're (stochastically?) parroting Claude to win brownie points, that's probably because you're not actually going deep.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngugdmmlu1vv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgugdmmlu1vv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I have nothing against people who consult LLMs to craft high-quality applications that read like they were written by a human expert. The “obviously AI” bit is genuinely load-bearing. And here’s the kicker — it quietly makes my eyes bleed.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0c6xgiusiag5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0c6xgiusiag5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Obviously there's nothing intrinsically wrong with not speaking English. But English is the language that almost all of the existing literature is in, and the common tongue of the field. An LLM translator can only do so much.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfdutvpq9dko"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffdutvpq9dko">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For future rounds, I should probably make it a requirement for applicants to disclose AI use in the application (and what they used it for).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvilspag2h87"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvilspag2h87">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As with many things, the exact count is complicated. Some people were obvious scammers. Some asked if they were out of scope and I said yes. And some I rejected early because they obviously weren’t going to be funded. I only evaluated 93 with Claude’s help.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntnhw8t7i9hm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftnhw8t7i9hm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Because I did a straightforward email export, Claude was able to see how I responded to the person via email, which did leak some bits about my opinion of them in some cases. But most of the time I just said “I’ll keep your application in mind.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7bdrlk32xbt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7bdrlk32xbt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In one case the pipeline failed for dumb reasons and I did my best to recreate it on the web chat interface. I don’t think it made much difference.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk8hatp1g7eo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk8hatp1g7eo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Nothing wrong with numbers. I love numbers! I just want the numbers to help me understand things, rather than inserted to impress </span><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2079717885258387456" rel="noreferrer"><span>the Grader</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnck5ww2q5sk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefck5ww2q5sk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It ran in a sandbox of course. And thankfully, sandboxes are always flawless. 🙃</span></p><p><span>More seriously, I am pretty confident that there weren’t any prompt injection attacks or other shenanigans from any applicants. It wouldn’t have changed the bottom line if there were, since I only used Claude as a sanity check. But also, nothing showed up on radar when I looked.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6w0jsy6v54s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6w0jsy6v54s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Send me an email at </span><a href="mailto:max@intelligence.org"><span>max@intelligence.org</span></a><span> if you want to join. I reserve the right to gatekeep.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q2YL7qKigC9QEEdsX/how-i-m-evaluating-corrigibility-grant-applications#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Bliki: Paracelsus Maxim](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/ParacelsusMaxim.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><b>The difference between a medicine and a poison is dosage.</b></p>

<p>Often we talk about certain habits, in programming or life, are good or
  bad. But few things are simple binaries. Some vary with context: reading a
  book is a good thing sitting in my garden, but not while driving my car. But
  another variable is dosage: a little pain-killer salves my headache, but too
  much will kill me.</p>

<p>The importance of dosage was noticed by a 16th century Swiss physician
  called Paracelsus. His quote was originally in German “Alle Dinge sind Gift,
  und nichts ist ohne Gift; allein die Dosis macht, dass ein Ding kein Gift
  ist.” which (<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_dose_makes_the_poison">according to
  Wikipedia</a>) translates as “All things are poison, and nothing is without
  poison; the dosage alone makes it so a thing is not a poison.” It's also known
  as “The dose makes the poison” or if you prefer your sayings in Latin “dosis
  sola facit venenum”.</p>

<p>In programming, global data is a good example of the Paracelsus Maxim (as I
  like to call it). A little global data, especially when immutable, can be a
  handy way of propagating information that may needed anywhere in a program,
  but it quickly becomes dangerous if there is a lot of it about.</p>

<p>This kind of thing crops up in lots of places. So when thinking about when
  things are good or bad, we should always ask “in what contexts” and “in what
  doses”?</p>

### [Maybe We Shouldn't Be Reviewing All This Code](https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/code-review.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p class="precis"><b>TL;DR</b><br /><i>Or, perhaps the problem isn't that AI has broken code review, maybe it’s that we've been using code review to solve the wrong problems</i></p>
<img src="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/card.png" /><p>I was on a panel recently with Brian Houck from DX at Code Remix, hosted by Moderne. It was one of the more interesting panels I’ve done, largely because we disagreed. As my colleague Martin Fowler says, panels are much more interesting when people disagree and both sides have a good argument. Brian and I definitely did.</p>

<p>Brian has since written a thoughtful piece called <a href="https://newsletter.getdx.com/p/what-are-code-reviews-even-for"><em>What are code reviews even for?</em></a> He is clearly passionate about his position, and I am passionate enough about mine that I’m writing this response. To be clear, I think we mostly want the same things. I just don’t think code review is the best way to get them. Brian is lovely, by the way, and encouraged me to write this. But I’d be lying if I said I didn’t want you to think I’m right by the end :)</p>

<p>So what were we disagreeing about?</p>

<p>AI is producing more code than humans can realistically review. Brian cites some pretty striking numbers: at Meta, significant lines of code per human-landed diff reportedly increased 106% in a year, while DX’s own data shows median pull request size increasing 64%.</p>

<p>His concern, which I share, is that simply automating code review away risks losing all the other things we use it for. Code review isn’t just about finding bugs. It’s how teams share knowledge, teach junior engineers, build collective ownership and spread architectural understanding.</p>

<p>My question is: <strong>why are we waiting until code review to do all of those things?</strong></p>

<p>I’ve never particularly liked pull requests as the centre of the software development process. Not because engineers shouldn’t look at each other’s code, but because I’ve always struggled with the idea that we should build something, finish it, package it up, throw it over to somebody else and <em>then</em> have the important conversation about whether we built the right thing in the right way.</p>

<p>And don’t even get me started on merge conflicts. I’ve lost too many hours of my life.</p>

<h2 id="shift-the-judgment-left"><strong>Shift the judgment left</strong></h2>

<p>One of the principles I learned very early at Thoughtworks was to shorten feedback loops. If feedback is valuable, don’t remove it. Move it closer to the decision it is informing.</p>

<p>Take the things we say code review gives us.</p>

<p>If we want to <strong>explore alternative solutions</strong>, I’d rather do that before implementing one of them.</p>

<p>If we want <strong>knowledge transfer</strong>, pair. Sitting next to someone, physically or virtually, while they reason through a problem teaches you far more than reading their completed solution afterwards.</p>

<p>If we want <strong>junior engineers to learn how experienced engineers think</strong>, let them work with experienced engineers while they’re thinking. Pairing comes to mind again here, but teams could also do design sessions collectively with a whiteboard before they write (or instruct the agent to write) anything.</p>

<p>If we want <strong>collective ownership</strong>, organise teams so people actually build and operate software collectively rather than relying on a pull request to tell everyone what somebody else has already built. For this again use pairing, mob programming, or team design sessions around whiteboard.</p>

<p>If we want <strong>architectural alignment</strong>, design together (I won’t repeat myself about pairing and team design sessions, oh wait…) and then encode the important constraints as fitness functions.</p>

<p>And if we’re reviewing code for formatting, linting, known security problems or things that can be deterministically tested, automate them. We really shouldn’t still be arguing about whitespace in 2026.</p>

<p>Pair programming, trunk-based development, automated testing, static analysis, fitness functions and security scanning all move feedback earlier. Increasingly, agents can participate in those loops too, challenging designs, testing assumptions and continuously verifying what is being built, but the real thinking is coming from experienced humans and if we want that experience to benefit the whole team then we have to act like one much earlier than code review.</p>

<h2 id="review-by-exception"><strong>Review by exception</strong></h2>

<p>None of this means nobody ever reviews code. There are absolutely changes where I want another experienced human looking. An example would be a fundamental architectural change. Assuming we did a design session as a wider team, we might want to review the code as a team or agree it was implemented right, or discuss if we want to change anything. Other examples could be something crossing a sensitive security boundary, a change with a huge blast radius, an unfamiliar part of a critical system or simply something where the team says, “I’m not confident about this.”</p>

<p>Those are exactly the places where human judgment is valuable, but that’s very different from requiring a human to inspect every change because that’s the ceremony we’ve historically used to create confidence.</p>

<p>And we know now it’s not viable to continue down this path, hence why code review keeps coming up as an issue or a blocker. If an agent can produce ten times the code but every line eventually queues up waiting for a senior engineer to inspect it, we haven’t created a ten-times engineering organisation, we’ve created a big backlog and a new bottleneck.</p>

<p>And I don’t think the answer is an AI agent pretending to be the human reviewer so we can preserve exactly the same process at higher speed. That’s automating the ceremony rather than questioning why the ceremony exists.</p>

<p>There is one thing I do worry about in Brian’s argument, though. He talks about teams accumulating cognitive and intent debt: software grows while the humans responsible for it understand less and less about why it works the way it does. I think that’s a very real problem. I just don’t think mandatory pull requests are a particularly strong defence against it.</p>

<p>If agents are going to produce substantially more of the implementation, we need to be much more deliberate about maintaining human understanding through collaborative design, pairing, good boundaries, executable architecture, shared operational responsibility and probably some practices we haven’t invented yet.</p>

<p><strong>We need engineers to understand systems, not diffs.</strong></p>

<p>Perhaps that’s what AI is exposing. We’ve spent years loading an extraordinary number of responsibilities onto the humble code review: quality gate, security check, architecture review, mentoring mechanism, knowledge-sharing system, ownership model.</p>

<p>It worked, sort of, while humans could only produce code so quickly. That constraint is disappearing. So perhaps the question isn’t how we get the code reviewed faster. Perhaps it’s why we’re waiting until code review to have all the important conversations in the first place.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September  1](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-01.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Like many readers, I’m wary of AI generated prose. Simon Wilison has written an <a href="https://tools.simonwillison.net/llm-cliche-highlighter#https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html">LLM cliché highlighter</a> - paste in some text, or a URL, and it will flag various patterns common to LLMs. It references a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing">wikipedia page of signs of AI writing</a>. That page points out that:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Humans are notoriously bad at distinguishing human and LLM-generated text. While research on humans’ abilities to detect AI-generated text is still limited, a 2025 study has shown that human ability to distinguish LLM text from human is no better than random chance. Another 2025 study on German theses has shown that humans managed a “recognition rate of 57% for AI texts and 64% for human-generated texts”.[</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Not just do I find myself repelled by prose with an LLM-voice, I also wonder how accurate my reaction is. I’m old enough to see all sorts of new tic-phrases appear, and in the past would just chalk it up to youngsters or airport business books. (Not to mention Americanisms, which I’ll get used to momentarily.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>NVIDIA’s technical blog reports on an <a href="https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-avo-reaches-100-on-arc-agi-3-demonstrating-a-frontier-level-general-purpose-architecture-for-long-horizon-autonomous-agents/">Architecture for Long-Horizon Autonomous Agents</a>. Their research group used a combination of Claude Opus 5 and a harness called AVO, and used it first to do GPU kernel optimization and then a broader reasoning benchmark (ARC-AGI-3). Both of these were long-term tasks, for the kernel optimization the agent ran for seven days.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>AVO is designed to preserve progress beyond a single model context. Two mechanisms are particularly important: persistent memory and supervision.</p>

  <p>Persistent memory carries forward prior implementations, evaluation results, compiler and profiler outputs, and accumulated reasoning, allowing the agent to resume from the current state rather than repeatedly reconstructing the search.</p>

  <p>The supervisor monitors the broader trajectory for stagnation or repeated unproductive cycles and can redirect the main agent toward alternative strategies when needed. During the seven-day attention-kernel run, the main agent remained responsible for deciding what to inspect, change, test, and evaluate, while the supervisor helped maintain forward progress when the search plateaued.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The team was encouraged that AVO did well at two different kinds of long-horizon tasks, indicating that it’s a general-purpose tool.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/mickeynp/status/2092525399209058394">Mickey Petersen</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>MCP is SOAP for Zoomers.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Paul Stack writes that <a href="https://stack72.dev/ai-broke-the-assumptions-behind-ci/">AI Broke the Assumptions Behind CI</a>. Here’s his description of CI with agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An agent writes a change, opens a PR, and CI picks it up instantly. The compile fails, the agent pushes a fix, CI picks it up instantly again. A test fails, another fix, another instant run. Each iteration is fast, but the agent is still discovering that its change doesn’t work only after it crosses the PR boundary. The feedback loop is in the wrong place regardless of how fast CI runs.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that all of this breaks the pipeline, because “CI” keeps failing, and advocates doing verification before the agent pushes. This is where I get to be the grumpy old guy, and point out that was <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/continuousIntegration.html#BuildingAFeatureWithContinuousIntegration">always how Continuous Integration works</a>. When I’m done with a change, first I pull (to get everyone else’s change since I started), I build and test locally, and if all is well I push and let the CI server do its thing. The only reason the CI server should fail is if there’s some funky mismatch between my machine and the CI server. Tests that take a while to run aren’t part of this loop, instead they are run further down the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DeploymentPipeline.html">deployment pipeline</a>, downstream of CI. Any failures there imply missing tests in CI.</p>

<p>(I’m being a bit unfair dumping on this article here. After all I could have filled a full working day correcting misleading descriptions of Continuous Integration for most of the last twenty years. Maybe I’m just after an excuse to point readers to the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/delivery.html">extensive range of articles</a> hosted here about what’s needed to get code from laptop to production.)</p>

<p>Stack is right that we should question how the deployment pipelines should work with agents in play. He’s also right that CI with humans relies on them being disciplined to run commit tests locally before pushing to the CI server - and that we can (and should) automate that when using agents. I also don’t know more about his setup than what he’s written in his post, so there’s likely complications he faces that I don’t understand. But when thinking about designing pipelines it’s important to understand the principles that underlie <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/ContinuousDelivery.html">Continuous Delivery</a>, understand how the practices really work, and understand why they are in place. Above all, Continuous Integration is a practice, not just the CI server. Yes, CI does conflate two jobs: executing verification and coordinating merges. But that’s the point: verification is a necessary part of merging if we want to retain a healthy <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/branching-patterns.html#mainline">mainline</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Recently Noah Smith posted an article about how he was worried about an <a href="https://www.noahpinion.blog/p/heres-how-were-all-going-to-die">AI-generated super-virus savaging humanity</a>. It’s a worry I’ve heard a few times, seen as a greater concern than AI turning us into labradors or paper-clips. Claus Wilke, who works in the field, <a href="https://blog.genesmindsmachines.com/p/im-sorry-youre-not-going-to-die-from">isn’t so concerned</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Computational design of biological systems is unfathomably difficult. Experts who have dedicated their life to this topic routinely hit their head against the wall when nothing they try seems to work. PhD students in 2026 using state-of-the-art AI software are spending months or years trying to design simple peptide binders that inhibit some enzyme or pull down some protein, and the majority of their designs fail, or don’t express, or are toxic. But in Smith’s fictitious world a disgruntled teenager with no special training in biology can just solve a problem thousands of times more complicated than designing a peptide binder. The distance between where we are today and where we would have to be for Smith’s story to have any realism is enormous.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>It seems that a couple of remarkably talented  academics are experts in a staggeringly wide range of fields. <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.02184">Or maybe they are just ghosts.</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>These names do not exist. Elena Vasquez and Marcus Chen have appeared as volcano experts, astronauts, thriller protagonists, podcast hosts, and academic co-authors across hundreds of independently produced AI-generated documents, never having lived. We show that large language models do not merely default to high-probability individual names when generating fictional experts: they produce correlated character ensembles: pairs and trios whose co-occurrence rates far exceed chance and are consistent across independent generations.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Making Your Data Ready for Agentic AI](https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

      their processes, save money, and juice margins. But AI's capabilities are
      founded on the data that AI accesses, and for many organizations that
      foundation is little more than sand. <b class="author">Pramod Sadalage</b> and
      <b class="author">Prem Chandrasekaran</b> write about how to build a reliable
      foundation of data that can be accurate and trusted.</p>
